\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})}\Pr\left[\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}\left(\partial\mathcal{E}\right)\right]\ .

(78)

Let
\mathcal{E}\subset E
be arbitrary and set
\mathcal{M}:=\mathsf{MinMatch}\left(\partial\mathcal{E}\right)
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{M}\ .

(79)

We claim that at least half of the edges of
\mathcal{R}

do not belong to
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}\right|\geq|\mathcal{R}|/2\ .

(80)

For the sake of contradiction, assume that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}\right|<|\mathcal{R}|/2\ .

(81)

Then the set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{M}
is a matching
of
\partial\mathcal{E}
since

\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\partial\left(\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{M}\right)

(82)

\displaystyle=\partial\mathcal{R}\oplus\partial\mathsf{MinMatch}\left(\partial\mathcal{E}\right)

(83)

\displaystyle=\partial\mathcal{E}\ ,

(84)

where we used that every
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
has trivial boundary
\partial\mathcal{R}=\emptyset
.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{R}|

(85)

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{M}|-|\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{R}|

(86)

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}|+|\mathcal{M}|-(|\mathcal{R}|-|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}|)

(87)

\displaystyle=(2|\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}|-|\mathcal{R}|)+|\mathcal{M}|

(88)

\displaystyle<|\mathcal{M}|\ ,

(89)

where we used our assumption (
81
). Thus
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is
a matching of
\partial\mathcal{E}
of smaller cardinality than
\mathcal{M}
, contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{M}
is a minimum matching of
\partial\mathcal{E}
.

Eq. (
80
) shows that
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{M}
together with
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
imply that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}|

\displaystyle\geq|\mathcal{R}|/2\ .

(90)

Indeed, the fact that
\mathcal{R}
is contained in the symmetric difference

\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{M}
implies that
\mathcal{R}
is the disjoint union
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E})\cup(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{M})

and thus
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}\supseteq\mathcal{R}\backslash\mathcal{M}
. This implies the claim (
90
) together with (
80
).

With (
90
), we conclude that for every
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
, we have

|\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}|\geq\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil
(because
|\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}|
is an integer) and thus

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}\left(\partial\mathcal{E}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[|\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}|\geq|\mathcal{R}|/2\right]

(91)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\exists\delta\subseteq\mathcal{R}\ \textrm{ with }|\delta|=\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil\textrm{ and }\delta\subseteq\mathcal{E}\right]\ .

(92)

Here we used that for any set
\mathcal{E}
with
|\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}|\geq\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil
, there is a subset
\delta\subseteq\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{E}
of size exactly equal to
\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil
.
We can sum over subsets
\delta\subset\mathcal{R}
of cardinality
|\delta|=\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil
to obtain with the union bound

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}\left(\partial\mathcal{E}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\subseteq\mathcal{R}\\
|\delta|=\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil\end{subarray}}\Pr\left[\delta\subseteq\mathcal{E}\right]

(93)

\displaystyle\leq\binom{|\mathcal{R}|}{\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil}\cdot p^{\lceil|\mathcal{R}|/2\rceil}\ ,

(94)

where we used that
\mathcal{E}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
, i.e., the fact that
\mathcal{E}
is local stochastic. The claim follows by inserting this into (
78
) and applying the definition of
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}}(p)
.
∎

5
Single-shot decoding under local stochastic noise

In this section, we establish our main result about single-shot decoding of the 2D surface code.
We consider a situation where a surface-code encoded state is corrupted by a local stochastic error, and where the output state
\rho_{\mathrm{out}}
is obtained by running Algorithm
1
on this corrupted logical state.

In Section
5.1
, we consider a half-encoded Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
defined on the surface code system
\mathcal{C}
of the surface code and an auxiliary qubit system
R
. Supposing that Algorithm
1
is executed on the surface code system of the Bell state corrupted by a Pauli error
E
on the surface code qubits, we show that the output state then is one of the Bell basis states
\{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}\}_{\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}}
, and find corresponding expressions for
\alpha
and
\beta
.

In Section
5.2
, we compute upper bounds on the resilience function of the recovery sets
\mathcal{L}_{X}
and
\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}
.
Using these bounds and the expressions for
(\alpha,\beta)
obtained in Section
5.1
,
we prove in Section
5.3
that for any input state corrupted by a local stochastic error of strength
p
below a certain threshold, the overlap
\expectationvalue{\rho_{\mathrm{out}}}{\Psi}
between
\Psi
and the output state
\rho_{\mathrm{out}}
is at least
1-O(p)
.

5.1
Decoding on a corrupted half-encoded Bell state

In the following, we use the logical Pauli operators

\displaystyle\overline{X}

\displaystyle=X(\mathcal{L}_{X})X_{q}

(95)

\displaystyle\overline{Z}

\displaystyle=Z(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{Z})Z_{q}\

(96)

of the surface code.
We consider the action of Algorithm
1
on a certain bipartite state of the form
\Phi_{\mathcal{C}R}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}\cup\{R\}}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, where
R
is a reference qubit. Specifically, we are interested in the Bell state
\Phi_{\mathcal{C}R}
between a surface-code encoded logical qubit and the reference qubit
R
.
We call this state the half-encoded Bell state (since the qubit
R
is “physical”, i.e., not encoded). The state has stabilizer generators
\{A_{v}\}_{v}\cup\{B_{f}\}_{f}\cup\{S^{X},S^{Z}\}
, where
\{A_{v}\}_{v}

and
\{B_{f}\}_{f}
are the usual surface code generators (associated with vertices and faces of the surface code lattice), and where

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}S^{X}&:=&\overline{X}X_{R}&=&X(\mathcal{L}_{X})X_{q}X_{R}\\
S^{Z}&:=&\overline{Z}Z_{R}&=&Z(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{Z})Z_{q}X_{R}\ ,\end{matrix}

(97)

are the Bell state stabilizer generators. In addition to the stabilizer generators
\{S^{X},S^{Z}\}
, the following sets
\{S^{u}\}_{u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}}
and
\{S^{u^{*}}\}_{u^{*}\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}
of stabilizers of the state
\Phi_{\mathcal{C}R}
play a special role in our argument. They are defined as follows: Any internal vertex
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
is associated with it a stabilizer generator
S^{u}
of the surface code consisting of Pauli-
X
-operators only, namely
S^{u}=A_{u}
.
In a similar vein, any internal vertex
u^{*}\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
gives rise to a stabilizer
S^{u^{*}}
consisting of
Z
-type operators, namely the face operator
B_{u^{*}}
when
u^{*}
is considered as a face of the original surface code lattice
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}
.
Observe that for each
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
,
the support
\mathrm{supp}(S^{u})=\mathrm{Inci}(u)
of
S^{u}
is
equal to the set of edges incident on
u
in the graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
. Similarly, for each
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, we have
\mathrm{supp}(S^{u})=\mathrm{Inci}^{*}(u)
where
\mathrm{Inci}^{*}(u)
is the set of edges incident on
u
in the graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
. That is, we have

\displaystyle S^{u}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}(u)}X_{v}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}\\
\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}(u)}Z_{v}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\ .\end{cases}

(98)

Consider the result of running the algorithm on a corrupted state

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\textrm{in}}}

\displaystyle=E\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}\

(99)

for a fixed Pauli error
E
.
We assume here that
\mathrm{supp}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{C}
, i.e., the error acts on the surface code qubits only (and does not affect the reference system
R
). Let

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(x,z,E)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p(x,z|E)}}\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q,R\}}\right)E\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}\

(100)

be the state obtained after Step
2
of the algorithm on qubit
q
.
Here we denote by
p(x,z|E)
the probability of obtaining the outcomes
(x,z)
given a Pauli error
E
, i.e., for the input state (
99
).
It will be convenient in the following to use the definition

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(P,Q)=\begin{cases}0&{ if $P$ and $Q$ commute}\\
1&{ otherwise }\end{cases}

(101)

for any two Pauli operators
P
and
Q
.
Then the following holds:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
be the operators (
97
). Let

\displaystyle c_{X}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

(102)

\displaystyle c_{Z}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(103)

Then the post-measurement state
\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(x,z,E)
on the two qubits
q,R

after Step
2

of Algorithm
1
, given an initial error
E
and measurement outcomes
(x,z)
is the Bell state (cf. (
12
))

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(x,z,E)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\Phi_{(c_X,c_Z)}}\ .

(104)

Proof.

The claim is an immediate consequence of the fact that
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
are stabilizers of
\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
,
as well as the expressions (
97
) of the stabilizer generators: Replacing
\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
in (
100
) by
S^{X}\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
, we observe that the post-measurement state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(x,z,E)}

satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{p}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{q}\right)ES^{X}\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}

(105)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{q}\otimes I_{R}\right)S^{X}E\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}

(106)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes X_{q}X_{R}\right)E\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}\ ,

(107)

where
p:=p(x,z|E)
and where we used (
97
) as well as the identity

\displaystyle X(\mathcal{L}_{X})H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}_{\mathcal{X}}\ .

(108)

We conclude that

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}X_{q}X_{R}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

(109)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{c_{X}}X_{q}X_{R}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}\ ,

(110)

by definition of
c_{X}
.

In an analogous manner, we can check that
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}=(-1)^{c_{Z}}Z_{q}Z_{R}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}
by
replacing

\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
in (
100
) by
S^{Z}\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
. Thus the two-qubit state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}
on systems
q,R
is stabilized by
\{(-1)^{c_{X}}X_{q}X_{R},(-1)^{c_{Z}}Z_{q}Z_{R}\}
. This implies the claim.
∎

According to Lemma
5.1
, the post-measurement state is fully determined by the single-qubit measurement results
(x,z)
and the bits
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)
and
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
.
To analyze these bits, we introduce decomposition

\displaystyle E=E^{X}E^{Z}

(111)

of
E
into a product of Pauli-
X
and Pauli-
Z
operators
E^{X}
and
E^{Z}
, respectively. (We note that if
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
is a local stochastic error of strength
p
, the same is true for both
E^{X}
and
E^{Z}
, i.e.,
E^{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
and
E^{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
, a fact we will use below.)

In (
111
), the global phase is ignored since we will only need the supports
\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})
and
\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})
.
We show that the bits
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)
and
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
are determined by the physical errors on qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
as well as a certain restriction of the error as specified in the following statement:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
E
be a Pauli operator on
\mathcal{C}
and let
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
be the Pauli operators defined by (
97
). Decompose
E
into a product
E=E^{X}E^{Z}
of Pauli-
X
- and Pauli-
Z
-operators, respectively.
Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{X}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(112)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(113)

Proof.

Let
E
be an arbitrary Pauli operator on
\mathcal{C}
.
With expression (
97
) for
S^{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}\left(X_{q}X_{R}X\left(\mathcal{L}_{X}\right),E\right)

(114)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{X}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(115)

since
E
acts trivially on the reference system
R
. The claim for
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
follows similarly.
∎

Unfortunately, the operators
E^{X}
and
E^{Z}
are not determined by the measurement results
(x,z)
. In particular, the bits
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)
and
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
cannot be computed from the measurement outcomes. Algorithm
1
therefore generates estimates
\hat{s}^{X}
and
\hat{s}^{Z}
for these bits, see step
5
.

To motivate the definition of these bits, and to derive a sufficient condition for when the algorithm is correct (i.e., generates the Bell state
\Phi
), we discuss how the measurement results
(x,z)
constrain the error
E
, or more precisely
the operators
E^{X}
and
E^{Z}
. That is, we consider what information can be gathered from these measurement outcomes. Observe that the operators
(E^{X},E^{Z})
are fully determined by the supports
\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})
and
\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})
.
We obtain constraints on these sets expressed in terms of the strings
s,s^{*}
computed in Step
3
of Algorithm
1
.

Lemma 5.3
.

The strings
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}}
and
s^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}
computed (from the measurement outcomes
(x,z)
) in Step
3
satisfy

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})

(116)

\displaystyle s^{*}

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})\ .

(117)

Proof.

Let
s=(s_{u})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}}
be the string computed in step
3
, i.e.,
s=\partial x
. Let
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
.
Since
s_{u}=(\partial x)_{u}=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

and
\left(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\right)_{u}=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle
, we need to show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\qquad\textrm{ for every }\qquad u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}\ .

(118)

Note that
S^{u}
stabilizes the (uncorrupted) state
\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
(by definition of the latter).
Replacing
\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
in (
100
) by
S^{u}\ket{\Phi_{\cC R}}
yields

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q,R\}}\right)ES^{u}W\ket{0^\cC}

(119)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)}\left(\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q,R\}}\right)S^{u}EW\ket{0^\cC}

(120)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}\ .

(121)

Here the third equality follows from the identity

\displaystyle S^{u}H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}\

(122)

which is a consequence of
the definition of
S^{u}
(as an
X
-type
stabilizer generator the surface code), as well as the fact that for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, the set
\mathrm{supp}(S^{u})
does not contain
q
for any
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, see Eq. (
98
).
Identity (
121
)
implies that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad u\in V_{dec}^{int}\ .

(123)

Observe that according to (
98
), we also have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}\left(\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}(u)}X_{v}\right),E\right)=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\qquad\textrm{ for any }\qquad u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}\ .

(124)

Combining (
123
) with (
124
) gives the claim (
118
).

The claim for
s^{*}
can be proved in an analogous manner using (
98
).
∎

We now argue that the sets
m\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
m^{*}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
computed
in Step
4

of Algorithm
1
can be considered to be proxys for the sets
\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
. More precisely, we may define
Pauli errors

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\widehat{E}^{X}&=&X(m^{*})\\
\widehat{E}^{Z}&=&Z(m)\ .\end{matrix}

(125)

We note that by definition of
m
and
m^{*}
as boundaries of subsets of edges in the graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, respectively, and the definition of these graphs, neither
m
nor
m^{*}
contains the qubit
q
. Thus

\displaystyle\{q\}\cap\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{X})

\displaystyle=\{q\}\cap\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{Z})=\emptyset\ .

(126)

We trivially have

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{Z})

(127)

\displaystyle m^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{X})

(128)

by the definition (
125
) of these operators, and it follows immediately from the
definition of
m
and
m^{*}

that
(\widehat{E}^{X},\widehat{E}^{Z})

satisfy constraints analogous to the constraints (
116
), (
117
) obeyed by
(E^{X},E^{Z})
, i.e., we have

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\partial\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{Z})

(129)

\displaystyle s^{*}

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}^{X})\ .

(130)

Thus
\widehat{E}:=\widehat{E}^{X}\widehat{E}^{Z}
is an error consistent with the observed syndrome
(s,s^{*})
with the property that

\widehat{E}^{Z}
and
\widehat{E}^{X}

each are minimum weight matchings in
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, respectively.

Because the error
\widehat{E}
can be computed from the measurement outcomes, this motivates using the pair
\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},\widehat{E}),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},\widehat{E})\right)
as an estimate for

\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\right)
. Indeed, this is the reasoning underlying the definition

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\hat{s}^{X}&:=&\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{X}},{m}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\\
\hat{s}^{Z}&:=&\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}},{m^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\end{matrix}

(131)

in Step
5
of the algorithm: It is easy to check that
(\hat{s}^{X},\hat{s}^{Z})
defined in this way satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\hat{s}^{X}&=&\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},\widehat{E})\\
\hat{s}^{Z}&=&\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},\widehat{E})\ \end{matrix}

(132)

because of Expressions (
112
), (
113
): Indeed, we have for example

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},\widehat{E})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}\left(X(\mathcal{L}_{X})X_{q}X_{R},\widehat{E}^{X}\widehat{E}^{Z}\right)

(133)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{X}},{\mathrm{supp}(\hat{E}^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(134)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{X}},{m}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\

(135)

where we used the definition (
97
) in the first step, and the fact that
\hat{E}^{Z}
has no support on
\{q,R\}
in the second step (cf. (
126
)).
Identity (
132
) shows that the pair
(\hat{s}^{X},\hat{s}^{Z})
is a natural estimate for
the bits
\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\right)
, the idea being that
\widehat{E}
is a decent estimate of
E
.

Having motivated the algorithm, let us show that Algorithm
1
produces a Bell state
\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}
. Below, we will use the expressions for
(\alpha,\beta)
computed here below to analyze the success probability of the protocol (i.e., the probability that
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
) for local stochastic noise.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
E=E^{X}E^{Z}
be an arbitrary Pauli operator on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}}
.
Suppose Algorithm
1
is executed
with an auxiliary qubit system
R

and the initial state
\ket{\Psi_{\textrm{in}}}
specified by (
99
), i.e., the (half-encoded)
Bell state
\Phi_{\mathcal{C}R}
corrupted by the error
E
.
Then the output
of Algorithm
1
on qubits
q,R

is the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
, where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z}))},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\qquad\textrm{ and }

(136)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}^{*}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}))},{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(137)

Proof.

We first show that the output is the Bell state
\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}
with

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\oplus m},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\qquad\textrm{ and }

(138)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})\oplus m^{*}},{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(139)

where
m\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
m^{*}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
are the minimum matchings computed in Step
4
of the algorithm.

According to Lemma
5.1
, the
post-measurement state
after Step
2

of Algorithm
1
is the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(c_X,c_Z)}}

with

\displaystyle c_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

(140)

\displaystyle c_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(141)

Since the algorithm applies
the correction operation
Z^{\hat{c}_{X}}X^{\hat{c}_{Z}}

in Step
7
, the output of the algorithm is the Bell state
\ket{\Psi_{(c_X\oplus\hat{c}_X,c_Z\oplus\hat{c}_Z)}}
. It follows with the definition of
(\hat{c}_{X},\hat{c}_{Z})
in Step
6
,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{X}

(142)

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{Z}

(143)

that
the output is the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\hat{s}^{X}\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)\qquad\textrm{ and }

(144)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\hat{s}^{Z}\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(145)

The claim (
139
) now follows by combining the expressions (
112
), (
113
) for
\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\right)
from Lemma
5.2
with the
Definitions (
131
) of
(\hat{s}^{X},\hat{s}^{Z})
used in Step
5
of the algorithm.

The claim of the theorem now follows
by combining (
139
) with the fact that

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=\mathsf{MinMatch}(s)=\mathsf{MinMatch}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z}))

(146)

\displaystyle m^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{MinMatch}^{*}(s^{*})=\mathsf{MinMatch}^{*}(\partial^{*}\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}))\

(147)

by the definition of
(m,m^{*})
, and the expressions for
(s,s^{*})
established in Lemma
5.3
.
∎

5.2
Bounds on the resilience functions for
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

Here we establish the following bounds on the resilience functions of interest:

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider the graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
with internal vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
defined by (
54
), see Fig.
7a
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{X}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}
be the subset of edges on the vertical strip in Fig.
7a
(cf. (
50
)).
Then the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}(p)

\displaystyle\leq 54p

(148)

for any
p\in\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{144}\mathclose{]}
.

Proof.

Consider a simple closed loop
L\in Z_{\circ}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}})
.
Clearly, the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
implies that
L
and
\mathcal{L}_{X}
have no overlap.
In particular,
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{L},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=0
, implying that closed loops do not contribute to the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}
in this case.

To describe the set
Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}})
of simple paths with endpoints in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
through internal vertices, we label the external vertices in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{dec}}

\displaystyle=\left\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{d-1},v_{1},\ldots,v_{d-1}\right\}\ ,

(149)

see Fig.
7a
.
Explicitly, the external vertices are

\displaystyle u_{j}=(0,2j),\quad v_{j}=(2,2j)\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad j=\{1,\dots,d-1\}\ .

(150)

Clearly, a simple path
P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}})
with endpoints in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
through internal vertices satisfies
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{P},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1
if and only if

P
starts at
u_{j}
(with
j\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
) and ends at
v_{k}
(with
k\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
).
For
j\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Delta(u_{j},\ell)
be the set of all such paths
P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}})
of length
\ell
that start at
u_{j}
and end at some vertex in the set
\{v_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d-1}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}(p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\binom{\ell}{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\cdot|\bigcup_{j=1}^{d-1}\Delta(u_{j},\ell)|\cdot p^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}

(151)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\sum_{\ell=L^{\min}_{j}}^{L^{\max}_{j}}\binom{\ell}{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\cdot|\Delta(u_{j},\ell)|\cdot p^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\ .

(152)

because
\Delta(u_{j},\ell)
and
\Delta(u_{j^{\prime}},\ell)
are disjoint for
j\neq j^{\prime}
since correspondings paths have distinct starting points
u_{j}\neq u_{j^{\prime}}
.
Here
L^{\min}_{j}
and
L^{\max}_{j}
are the minimal and maximal lengths of a simple path
P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}})
starting at
u_{j}
and ending in
\{v_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d-1}
without touching any other external vertices.

The set
\Delta(u_{1},1)
consists of a single path, i.e.,
|\Delta(u_{1},1)|=1
. For
j\in\{2,\ldots,d-1\}
, we use that the graph
T_{dec}
has vertices of degree at most
4
, which implies that

\displaystyle|\Delta(u_{j},\ell)|\leq 3^{\ell-1}\qquad\textrm{ for all }\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{N}\ .

(153)

This is because any path
P\in\Delta(u_{j},\ell)
has
the same first edge (connected to
u_{j}
), and there are at most
3
choices for each of the remaining edges.) Furthermore, it is clear that

\displaystyle L_{j}^{\min}\geq j\ .

(154)

We conclude (where for
\ell>1
, we use the inequalities
p^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\leq p^{\ell/2}
and

\binom{\ell}{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}\leq 2^{\ell}
) that

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}(p)

\displaystyle\leq p+\sum_{j=2}^{d-1}\sum_{\ell=j}^{L_{j}^{\max}}2^{\ell}3^{\ell-1}p^{\ell/2}

(155)

\displaystyle\leq p+\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j=2}^{d-1}\sum_{\ell=j}^{\infty}q^{\ell}\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad q:=6\sqrt{p}

(156)

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}}{6}+\frac{q^{2}-q^{d}}{3(1-q)^{2}}

(157)

\displaystyle\leq p+\frac{4}{3}\left(q^{2}-q^{d}\right)

(158)

\displaystyle\leq p+\frac{4}{3}\cdot\left(6\sqrt{p}\right)^{2}

(159)

\displaystyle\leq 54p\ .

(160)

Here we used the fact
q\leq 6\sqrt{\frac{1}{144}}=\frac{1}{2}
and that
\frac{1}{1-q}\leq 2
.
∎

A similar bound applies to the dual decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Consider the dual graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with internal vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
defined by (
57
), see Fig.
7b
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
be the subset of edges on the horizontal strip in Fig.
7b
(cf. (
51
)).
Then the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}(p)

\displaystyle\leq 38p

(161)

for any
p\in\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{144}\mathclose{]}
.

The proof is analogous to that of Lemma
5.5
. We include it in Appendix
B
for completeness.

5.3
A bound on single-shot decoding under local stochastic noise

Consider a distance-
d
surface code of
\absolutevalue{\cC}=2d^{2}-2d+1
qubits with
d\geq 2
and logical operators
\overline{X}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{L}_{X}\cup\{q\}}X_{j}
and
\overline{Z}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{L}_{Z}\cup\{q\}}
.
Let
\ket{\overline{0}}
and
\ket{\overline{1}}
be code states to eigenvalues
+1
and
-1
of
\overline{Z}
. Let

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}V:&\mathbb{C}^{2}&\mapsto&\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)^{\otimes\mathcal{C}}\\
&\alpha\ket{0}+\beta\ket{1}&\mapsto&\alpha\ket{\overline{0}}+\beta\ket{\overline{1}}\ \end{matrix}

(162)

be an isometric encoding map, and let

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathcal{E}:&\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{2})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{B}(\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)^{\otimes\mathcal{C}})\\
&\rho&\mapsto&V\rho V^{\dagger}\end{matrix}

(163)

be an encoding channel associated with
V
.
For any state
\rho\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, the state
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
is the corresponding encoded (logical) state.

We describe what single-shot decoding protocol achieves in terms of its logical action. For this purpose, let us define the single-qubit
random Pauli channel

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\nu}(\rho):=\nu_{0}\rho+\nu_{1}X\rho X^{\dagger}+\nu_{2}Y\rho Y^{\dagger}+\nu_{3}Z\rho Z^{\dagger}\ .

(164)

for any probability distribution
\nu=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3})
,
\nu_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=0}^{3}\nu_{j}=1
.

For
p\in[0,1]
, let

E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
be a local stochastic error with parameter
p
. This defines a “noise channel”
\Lambda_{p}
by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\Lambda_{p}:&\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}})\\
&\rho&\mapsto&\Lambda_{p}(\rho):=\sum_{E_{0}\textrm{ Pauli on }(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}}}\Pr\left[E=E_{0}\right]E_{0}\rho E_{0}^{\dagger}\end{matrix}

(165)

be the CPTP map that applies the error
E
to a state. Finally, let
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\{q\}})
be the CPTP map corresponding to an ideal execution of Algorithm
1
. Then the following holds:

Theorem 5.7

(Single-shot decoding of a surface code)
.

Consider local stochastic noise as expressed by a CPTP map
\Lambda_{p}
of the form (
165
), with noise strength

\displaystyle p\leq\frac{1}{144}\ .

(166)

There is a probability distribution
\nu=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3})
over
(I,X,Y,Z)
such that the following holds:

(i)

Suppose we run algorithm
1
on the noisy encoded state
\Lambda_{p}\circ\mathcal{E}(\rho)
, where

\rho\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is an arbitrary single-qubit state.
Then the resulting output state
\mathcal{D}\circ\Lambda_{p}\circ\mathcal{E}(\rho)
on qubit
q
is the state
\mathcal{P}_{\nu}(\rho)
.

(ii)

We have
1-\nu_{0}\leq 94p
.

In particular, Theorem
5.7
implies that applying Algorithm
1

to any encoded pure state
\Lambda_{p}(V\outerproduct{\Psi}{\Psi}V^{\dagger})
corrupted by
\Lambda_{p}
yields an output state
\rho_{\textrm{out}}=\mathcal{D}\circ\Lambda_{p}\circ\mathcal{E}(\ket{\Psi}\!\bra{\Psi})
with overlap at least

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi}\rho_{\textrm{out}}\ket{\Psi}\geq\nu_{0}\geq 1-94p

(167)

with the original state.

Proof.

Let us denote by
L\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
the “logical qubit” to be encoded, and let
R\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
be a reference system. We consider the action of the noise
and the decoding protocol when applied to the two-qubit Bell state
\Phi_{RL}:=\Phi_{(0,0)}
, i.e., we compute the Choi-Jamiolkowski state

\displaystyle J_{Rq}:=(\mathsf{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{D}\circ\Lambda_{p}\circ\mathcal{E})(\Phi_{RL})\ .

(168)

Observe that
\Phi^{\prime}_{R\mathcal{C}}:=(\mathsf{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{E})(\Phi_{RL})
is the half-encoded Bell state by definition. It thus follows from Theorem
5.4
that

\displaystyle J_{Rq}:=(\mathsf{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\nu})(\ket{\Phi_{(0,0)}}\!\bra{\Phi_{(0,0)}})

(169)

for a probability distribution
\nu=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3})
over
(I,X,Y,Z)
. In this expression,
\nu_{0}
is the probability that the condition (
137
) is satisfied. Because of the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism, Eq. (
169
) shows that the channel
\mathcal{D}\circ\Lambda_{p}\circ\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{P}_{\nu}
is a random Pauli channel. It thus remains to establish an upper bound on
1-\nu_{0}
.

Let us introduce the following four random variables:

\displaystyle A_{Z}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(170)

\displaystyle B_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z}))},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(171)

\displaystyle A_{X}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(172)

\displaystyle B_{X}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}^{*}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}))},{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(173)

Then (
137
) translates to

\displaystyle\nu_{0}

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\textrm{ and }(A_{X},B_{X})\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\right]\ .

(174)

The union bound gives

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}\right]+\Pr\left[(A_{X},B_{X})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}\right]\ .

(175)

Again using the union bound, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})=(0,1)\right]+\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})=(1,0)\right]

(176)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]+\Pr\left[A_{Z}=1\right]\ .

(177)

Applying the same reasoning to
(A_{X},B_{X})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[A_{X}=1\right]+\Pr\left[A_{Z}=1\right]+\Pr\left[B_{X}=1\right]+\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]\ .

(178)

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[A_{Z}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1\right]

(179)

\displaystyle\leq p

(180)

where we used that
E^{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
is local stochastic noise with the same parameter
p
as
E
. By similar reasoning, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[A_{X}=1\right]

\displaystyle\leq p\ .

(181)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}(\partial\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z}))},{\mathcal{L}_{X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1\right]

(182)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}(p)

(183)

\displaystyle\leq 54p

(184)

where we used the definition of
A_{Z}
, the fact that
E^{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
and Proposition
4.3
, as well as the upper bound (
148
). Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[B_{X}=1\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{Z}^{*}}(p)

(185)

\displaystyle\leq 38p\ .

(186)

Combining these inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{0}

\displaystyle\leq p+p+38p+54p

(187)

\displaystyle\leq 94p

(188)

as claimed.
∎

6
Entanglement generation from a noisy 3D cluster state

In the following, we describe a protocol for generating a two-qubit Bell state in a noise-robust fashion. It is based on the cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
on a 3D lattice
\mathcal{C}
, a state which can be generated by a depth-
6
circuit
W
. This state is measured using single-qubit measurements on all but two qubits. Based on the measurement outcomes, a certain correction operation is applied to one of the remaining two qubits.

In Section
6.1
we discuss the relevant definitions underlying the cluster state. In Section
6.2

we describe the single-qubit measurement pattern used in the protocol. Section
6.3
introduces certain subgraphs of the cluster state lattice and additional data such as distinguished subsets of qubits having to do with the encoded logical information. These are relevant for the definition of certain decoding functions used in the protocol.

Section
6.4
identifies certain stabilizers of the graph state that are key to the construction and analysis of the entanglement generation protocol. We give a complete description of the protocol in Section
6.5
. Finally, in Section
6.6
, we compute the output state of this algorithm when it is run on a noisy cluster state
EW\ket{0^{\cC}}
which is corrupted by a Pauli error
E
. This will be used to analyze the success probability for local stochastic errors in Section
7
.

6.1
Cluster state: Definition

Our construction is based on a 3D cluster state. To introduce this state, we mostly follow the conventions of
[
4
]
but
use different conventions to describe so-called dangling edges. Contrary to Ref.
[
4
]
we will consider their degree-
1
endpoints as regular vertices.

Let
d\geq 2
and let
R\geq 3
be an odd integer. Consider the set

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}[d\times d\times R]:=\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\mid 0\leq u_{1}\leq 2d,~0\leq u_{2}\leq 2d-2,~1\leq u_{3}\leq R\}

(189)

(From the perspective of coding, the integers
d
and
R
are associated with the distance
d
of a surface code, and a “cluster state distance”
\frac{R+1}{2}
, respectively.) In the following, we simply write
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}[d\times d\times R]
.
Elements
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
will be referred to as sites.

Qubits are located at each site belonging to the subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]:=\{u\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\mid 1\leq u_{1}\leq 2d-1\}\setminus\left\{(o,o,o),(e,e,e)\right\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}

(190)

where each
e~(o)
refers to any even (odd) integer. The set
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]
is called the cluster state lattice of the form
d\times d\times R
. We will refer to a site
u\in\mathcal{C}
interchangeably as site
u
or qubit
u
.
In the following, we use the convention that e.g.,

\{(e,e,R)\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
denotes the set of all
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
with
u_{1},u_{2}
even and
u_{3}=R
.

Given a site
u\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, the set of nearest neighbors of
u
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{neigh}(u)=\left\{v\in\mathcal{C}\mid d(u,v)=1\right\}\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad d(u,v):=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\absolutevalue{u_i - v_i}\ ,

(191)

i.e., the nearest neighbors of
u
are associated with qubits at Manhattan distance
1
from
u
. Because elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\mathcal{C}
are excluded in (
191
), any qubit
u\in\mathcal{C}
has at most four nearest neighbor qubits.

The cluster state of interest is associated with the graph whose vertex set is
\mathcal{C}
, and where
the set of edges consists of pairs
\{u,v\}
with
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
, see Fig.
8
for an illustration of this graph.
It is defined as

\displaystyle W\ket{0^\cC}\ ,

(192)

where

\displaystyle W=H(\mathcal{C})\left(\prod_{\left\{u,v\right\}}CZ_{u,v}\right)H(\mathcal{C})\ ,

(193)

with the product taken over all edges of the graph, i.e., all pairs
u,v\in\mathcal{C}
such that
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
.
Here
CZ_{u,v}
denotes a controlled-
Z
gate on qubits
u
and
v
. By definition, the unitary
W
can be implemented using a depth-
6
circuit with nearest-neighbor gates. (Note that
our definition of the graph state differs from the more commonly used definition by an additional layer of Hadamard-gates.) The state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
is a stabilizer state with stabilizer group generated by

\displaystyle G_{u}=Z_{u}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}X_{v}\qquad\textrm{ with }\qquad u\in\mathcal{C}\ .

(194)

Figure 8
:
The graph associated with the three-dimensional cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
. Each qubit is located at a vertex (sphere) in
\mathcal{C}
. Measurement pattern used in protocol is illustrated with colors.

As in the case of surface codes, it will also need a dual description. For this, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}[d\times d\times R]
be the set of dual sites defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}[d\times d\times R]:=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\ |\ 1\leq u_{1}\leq 2d-1\textrm{ and }-1\leq u_{2}\leq 2d-1\textrm{ and }1\leq u_{3}\leq R\right\}\ .

(195)

Then we can equivalently express the location of qubits as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{*}=\{u\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\mid 0\leq u_{2}\leq r-1\}\setminus\{(o,o,o),(e,e,e)\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\ ,

(196)

i.e., we have
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{*}
.

6.2
Measurement pattern used in protocol

In this section, we describe the measurement pattern used in our protocol. We partition the set of qubits as
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\{(e,o,1),(o,e,1),(e,o,R),(o,e,R)\in\mathcal{C}\},

(197)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{B}.

(198)

The set
\mathcal{B}
is the union of
\{(e,o,1),(o,e,1)\}
, i.e., a subset of all qubits at the “front” layer (i.e., with
u_{3}=1
) of
\mathcal{C}
, and
\{(e,o,R),(o,e,R)\}
, a subset of all qubits at the “back” layer (i.e., with
u_{3}=R
) of
\mathcal{C}
. In prior work
[
48
,
4
]
,
no measurements were applied to these qubits; instead, long-range encoded entanglement between two surface codes of distance
d
associated with these front- and back-layer qubits was established.

The remaining qubits
\{(e,e,1)\}
in the front layer are omitted from
\mathcal{B}
but belong to
\mathcal{A}
. Similarly, qubits
\{(e,e,R)\}
in the back layer belong to
\mathcal{A}
. These are auxiliary qubits whose measurement (specified below) reveals information about the associated surface code stabilizers in the corresponding layer. Similarly, the measurement results associated with the remaining qubits of
\mathcal{A}
belonging to the “bulk” reveal information about the logical error of the encoded surface code state (or, in our scheme, the final two-qubit entangled state).

As in
[
48
,
4
]
, all qubits belonging to
\mathcal{A}
are measured in the
Z
-basis. Because our goal is to establish entanglement between two (physical) qubits, we additionally measure all qubits in
\mathcal{B}
, except for two qubits

\displaystyle q_{1}

\displaystyle:=(1,0,1)

(199)

\displaystyle q_{2}

\displaystyle:=(1,0,R)

(200)

belonging to the front and back layers, respectively. Our protocol generates a Bell state between these two qubits.

The measurement pattern for the front layer qubits of
\mathcal{B}
mirrors the single-shot decoding procedure for the surface code: Qubits belonging to the upper left triangular region are measured in the
X
-basis, whereas qubits situated inside the lower right triangular region are measured in the
Z
-basis. Qubits in the back layer of
\mathcal{B}
are measured with an analogous pattern. Formally, the sets of qubits in
\mathcal{B}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
measured in the
X
- respectively
Z
-basis are

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}\mid u_{2}\geq 1+u_{1}\}

(201)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}\mid u_{2}<1+u_{1}\}\ .

(202)

The measurement outcome will be denoted by
(a,x,z)
, where

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{A}}

(203)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}

(204)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{Z}}\

(205)

are the measurement results associated with the qubits belonging to
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
, respectively.
This completes the description of the measurements used in the protocol, see Fig.
8
.

6.3
Subgraphs, recovery graphs and internal vertices

In this section, we introduce various subgraph of the cluster state lattice. We also
define certain subsets of qubits that are relevant for our entanglement generation protocol.

Specifically, in Section
6.3.1
,
we introduce the “glued graph”
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}
,
as well as the “dual glued graph”
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}
. They are obtained by gluing together surface code graphs respectively dual surface code graphs with the cluster state lattice. These graphs are at the basis of our entanglement generation protocol, and clarify the relationship to the single-shot decoding procedure for the surface code discussed in Section
3
.

In Section
6.3.2
, we then introduce the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and its dual counterpart
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
. In Section
6.3.3
, we additionally define certain subsets of their edges, the so-called recovery sets. We also identify the associated internal vertices. Our entanglement generation protocol involves solving corresponding matching problems.

6.3.1
Glued graphs

We introduce certain graphs in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
and their dual in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
as discussed in
[
4
]
.
Those graphs are subgraphs or supergraphs of certain graphs that will be introduced in Section
6.3.2
.

We first define an “even graph”
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}})
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
The set
\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}=\mathsf{V}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}
of vertices has two disjoint subsets

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\{(e,e,e)\}\mid 2\leq u_{2}\leq 2d-2\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\qquad\textrm{ and }

(206)

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\left\{(e,e,1),(e,e,R)\right\}\mid u_{1}\not\in\{0,2d\}\}\cup\{(0,e,e)\}\cup\{(2d,e,e)\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\ .

(207)

Here we avoid the terminology of dangling edges used in
[
4
]
and instead collect endpoints of such edges using the vertex set
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}
.
We note that
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}
consists of vertices
u=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
in the “front” (
u_{3}=1
), “back” (
u_{3}=R
), “left” (
u_{1}=0
), or “right” (
u_{1}=2d
) boundary.
Any pair
u,v\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}
of vertices at distance
d(u,v)=2
is connected by an edge in
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
, except when
u,v
both belong to
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}
.
Other edges in
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
are of the form
\{u,v\}
with

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\{(e,e,1),(e,e,R)\}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad v=\begin{cases}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}+1)&{ if $u_{3}=1$}\\
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}-1)&{ if $u_{3}=R$} \ .\end{cases}

(208)

It is easy to check that these edges constitute all pairs of vertices
u,v\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}
satisfying
d(u,v)=1
.
In summary, the set of edges of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}^{2}\mid d(u,v)=2\textrm{ and }(u,v)\not\in\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}\times\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{even}}\right\}

(209)

\displaystyle\quad\cup\left\{\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}^{2}\mid d(u,v)=1\right\}\ .

(210)

The “odd graph”
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{odd}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{odd}})
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
is a dual version of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
. The set
\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\mathsf{V}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{odd}}\cup\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{odd}}
of vertices consists of two disjoint subsets

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\circ}_{\mathrm{odd}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\{(o,o,o)\}\mid u_{2}\not\in\{-1,r\},u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\qquad\textrm{ and }

(211)

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{odd}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\left\{(o,o,1),(o,o,R)\right\}\mid u_{2}\not\in\{-1,r\}\}\cup\{u\in\{(o,-1,o),(o,r,o)\}\mid u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\ .

(212)

We note that
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{odd}}
consists of vertices
u=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
in the “front” (
u_{3}=1
), “back” (
u_{3}=R
), “top” (
u_{2}=2d
), or “bottom” (
u_{2}=-1
) boundary.
The set of edges of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\{\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{2}\mid d(u,v)=2\textrm{ and }(u,v)\not\in\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{odd}}\times\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{odd}}\}\ .

(213)

The surface code graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
has vertices belonging to the set of primal sites
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
It has two connected components lying in the planes
u_{3}=1
and
u_{3}=R
. Each connected component is isomorphic to the surface code graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{sc}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{sc}})
introduced in Section
3.1
.
Let us simply write

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}\times\{1,R\}\

(214)

for this graph to emphasize this fact. In more detail, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{sc}}}\{(u_{1},u_{2},1)\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{sc}}}\{(u_{1},u_{2},R)\}\right)

(215)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{{\tiny\begin{matrix}\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{sc}}\end{matrix}}}\Bigl\{\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},1),(v_{1},v_{2},1)\right\}\Bigr\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{{\tiny\begin{matrix}\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{sc}}\end{matrix}}}\Bigl\{\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},R),(v_{1},v_{2},R)\right\}\Bigr\}\right)\ .

(216)

The graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}
similarly consists of two copies of the dual surface code graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}
located in the planes
u_{3}=1
and
u_{3}=R
, respectively, which we express as

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}^{*}}\times\{1,R\}\ .

(217)

Written out, this means that

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{sc}^{*}}}\{(u_{1},u_{2},1)\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{sc}^{*}}}\{(u_{1},u_{2},R)\}\right)

(218)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{{\tiny\begin{matrix}\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{sc}^{*}}\end{matrix}}}\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},1),(v_{1},v_{2},1)\right\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{{\tiny\begin{matrix}\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{sc}^{*}}\end{matrix}}}\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},R),(v_{1},v_{2},R)\right\}\right)\ .

(219)

Now we are able to introduce a certain “glued graph”
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{gl}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{gl}})
,
as well as a “dual glued graph”
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}=(\mathsf{V}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}},\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}})
, in terms of the subgraphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}
, see Fig.
9
.

(a)

The glued graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}
as a union of two subgraphs:
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
(gray) and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
(green).

(b)

The dual glued graph
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}
as a union of two subgraphs:
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}
(gray) and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}
(green).

Figure 9
:

The glued graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
and the dual glued graph
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}\cup\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}
.

The glued graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}
is obtained by attaching the surface graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
to the even graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
; here the two copies of the surface code
end up being connected to the rough front- and back boundaries of the even graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
. The graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}
is formally defined by setting

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}

(220)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}\ .

(221)

We denote this construction simply by

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}\ .

(222)

The “dual glued graph”
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}=(\mathsf{V}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}},\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}})
is defined by
gluing the dual surface graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}}
to the odd graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}\cup\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{sc}^{*}}\ .

(223)

6.3.2
Decoding subgraphs

In the following, we describe how to process the measurement results in order to determine a suitable correction operation on the post-measurement on qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
to obtain the Bell state
\Phi
.
More precisely, the correction is a Pauli operator of the form
Z^{\hat{c}_{X}}X^{\hat{c}_{Z}}
, where
\hat{c}_{X}
and
\hat{c}_{Z}
are computed from the single-qubit measurements results by a classical computation we describe below.
The computation of
\hat{c}_{X}
requires finding a minimal matching of a subset of marked vertices on a certain “decoding” graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with interior and exterior vertices.
The computation of
\hat{c}_{Z}
proceeds in a similar “dual” manner and involves a “dual decoding graph”
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, see Fig.
10
for an illustration of these graphs.

The graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
play a central role in our protocol: We will relate the edges of the graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with the measured qubits. We will argue how the measurement results determine certain subsets of edges in these two graphs. These subsets are used to obtain certain subsets of marked vertices (by taking boundaries). The pair
(\hat{c}_{X},\hat{c}_{Z})
is computed from these subsets of marked vertices.

(a)

\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}=\left(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\right)

subsets of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

color

measurement

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}

Z
-basis

\left(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\right)\cap\mathcal{A}

Z
-basis

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{X}

X
-basis

\left(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\right)\cap\mathcal{X}

X
-basis

(b)

\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}=\left(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\right)

subsets of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

color

measurement

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}

Z
-basis

(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\setminus\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z})\cap\mathcal{A}

Z
-basis

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{Z}

Z
-basis

(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\setminus\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z})\cap\mathcal{Z}

Z
-basis

Figure 10
:

The decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
as well as the dual decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
. External vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
(black spheres) and internal vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
(white spheres) are illustrated.
The tables describe in which basis qubits corresponding to the edges are measured.

The decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
is the result of gluing two copies of the subgraph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\subset\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}
(instead of two copies of the entire graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{sc}}
) to
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}
, see Fig.
10a
.
By construction, the resulting decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
is a subgraph of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}
. Concisely, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{even}}\cup\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{1,R\}\right)\subset\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{gl}}\ .

(224)

The dual decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}})
is a subgraph of the dual glued graph
\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}
and is defined in a similar manner by

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}\cup\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\times\{1,R\}\right)\subset\mathsf{T}^{*}_{\mathrm{gl}}\ ,

(225)

see Fig.
10b
.

We explain the relationship between the qubits that are measured and the edges of the graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
using a bijection

\displaystyle\Lambda:\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cup\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\to\mathcal{C}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}\ .

(226)

called the “labeling” map. The map
\Lambda
identifies each edge of the graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with the site of a qubit located at the midpoint of the edge or at one of the vertices adjacent to the edge: Let
e=\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cup\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
be one of edges of the graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
.
If
d(u,v)=2
, then
\Lambda
maps the edge
e
to the midpoint
\Lambda(e)=\frac{u+v}{2}
of the vertices
u
and
v
.
Otherwise, i.e., if
d(u,v)\neq 2
, then we must have
d(u,v)=1
and it follows that
e
belongs to
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, and is of the form

\displaystyle\{u,v\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\{(u_{1},u_{2},1),(u_{1},u_{2},2)\}&{ if $\{u_{3},v_{3}\}=\{1,2\}$}\\
\{(u_{1},u_{2},R-1),(u_{1},u_{2},R)\}&{ if $\{u_{3},v_{3}\}=\{R-1,R\}$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\textrm{ with }\qquad u_{1},u_{2}\textrm{ even }

(227)

as stated in (
208
) of Section
6.3.1
.
In this case, the edge
e
is mapped by
\Lambda
to the site
(u_{1},u_{2},1)
if
\{u_{3},v_{3}\}=\{1,2\}
or the site
(u_{1},u_{2},R)
otherwise.
In summary, we can write the map
\Lambda
as

\displaystyle\Lambda(\{u,v\})=\begin{cases}(u_{1},u_{2},1)&{ if $\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}$, $d(u,v)=1$ and $\{u_{3},v_{3}\}=\{1,2\}$}\\
(u_{1},u_{2},R)&{ if $\{u,v\}\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}$, $d(u,v)=1$ and $\{u_{3},v_{3}\}=\{R-1,R\}$}\\
\left(\frac{u_{1}+v_{1}}{2},\frac{u_{2}+v_{2}}{2},\frac{u_{3}+v_{3}}{2}\right)&{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(228)

It is easy to check that the map
\Lambda
is a bijection.

Using the labeling map
\Lambda
, we can identify each edge of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
or
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with one of the measured qubits, or equivalently, the site of the latter. We say an edge
e\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cup\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
is labeled by a site
w\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
if
\Lambda(e)=w
.

With the labeling map
\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathcal{X}&=&\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{1,R\})\\
\mathcal{Z}&=&\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\times\{1,R\})\\
\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})\cap\mathcal{A}&=&\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}})\\
\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}})\cap\mathcal{A}&=&\Lambda(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{odd}})\ .\end{matrix}

(229)

Sometimes we simply denote an edge
e
by the corresponding label
\Lambda(e)
, leaving the map
\Lambda
implicit. Following this convention Eq. (
229
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathcal{X}&=&\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{1,R\}\\
\mathcal{Z}&=&\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\times\{1,R\}\\
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}&=&\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}\\
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\cap\mathcal{A}&=&\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{odd}}\ .\end{matrix}

(230)

Because of the definitions (
224
)
and (
225
)
of the graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, this allows us to write the set of edges
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
as unions of pairwise disjoint subsets
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\cap\mathcal{A}
of the measured qubits
\mathcal{C}\setminus\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
, namely

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}&=&\mathcal{X}\cup\left(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)\\
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}&=&\mathcal{Z}\cup\left(\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)\end{matrix}

(231)

In our entanglement generation protocol, we will use (
231
) to
interpret the measurement outcomes
(a,x,z)\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{Z}}
as defining two subsets of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, respectively.

6.3.3
Recovery sets and internal vertices of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

We define recovery sets
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
to be used later, see Fig.
10
for an illustration. The role of these sets in the context of the graph state will be clarified below, see Lemma
6.2
.

The recovery set
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
consists of edges incident on degree-
1
vertices (so-called dangling edges in the terminology of
[
4
]
) on the left rough boundary of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.
That is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{(0,u_{2},u_{3}),(2,u_{2},u_{3})\}\ |\ u_{2}\geq 2,u_{2}\textrm{ even, }u_{3}\in\{1,R\}\right\}

(232)

\displaystyle\quad\cup\left\{\{(0,u_{2},u_{3}),(2,u_{2},u_{3})\}\mid u_{2},u_{3}\textrm{ even }\right\}\ ,

(233)

or more succinctly (labeling edges by sites with the map
\Lambda
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}

\displaystyle=\left\{(1,u_{2},u_{3})\ |\ u_{2}\geq 2,u_{2}\textrm{ even, }u_{3}\in\{1,R\}\right\}\cup\left\{(1,u_{2},u_{3})\ |\ u_{2}\geq 2,u_{2},u_{3}\textrm{ even }\right\}\ .

(234)

Observe that the first set in this union is simply
\mathcal{L}_{X}\times\{1,R\}
, i.e., it consists of two copies of the set
\mathcal{L}_{X}
introduced for the surface code in Section
3.4
.
We can also write this set as
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}
or equivalently as
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{X}
(by the fact that
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}=\mathcal{X}
) using the labels.
Similarly, the second set in the union (
234
) is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}
.
In summary, the recovery set
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
is the disjoint union

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}=\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}\right)\ .

(235)

The dual recovery set
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
consists of edges incident on degree-
1
vertices at the bottom of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
.
For every site
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\left\{(o,-1,o)\right\}\setminus\left\{q_{1},q_{2}\right\}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, there exists such an edge incident on the vertex at
(u_{1},1,u_{3})
; these edges are labeled by their midpoint
(u_{1},0,u_{3})
.
Such an edge belongs to
\mathcal{A}
if and only if
u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}
, and to
\mathcal{B}
otherwise.
In summary, the recovery set
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}
is the disjoint union

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A})\cup(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B})

(236)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\left\{(o,0,o)\right\}\mid u_{1}\not\in\{1,R\}\right\}

(237)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\left\{(o,0,o)\right\}\mid u_{1}\in\{1,R\}\right\}\setminus\left\{q_{1},q_{2}\right\}\ .

(238)

Finally, let us define the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\subset\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
of internal vertices of the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and similarly, the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\subset\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
of internal vertices of the dual decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
.
We call their complements
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}:=\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\backslash\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}:=\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\backslash\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
the sets of external vertices, see Fig.
10
.

For the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, the set of internal vertices is

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

\displaystyle=\left\{u\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}\ |\ \mathrm{neigh}(u)\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}\right\}\cup\left(\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\left\{1,R\right\}\right)\ .

(239)

The set of internal vertices of the graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
is defined similarly
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\left\{u\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{odd}}\ |\ \mathrm{neigh}(u)\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{odd}}\right\}\cup\left(\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\times\{1,R\}\right)\ ,

(240)

or equivalently as

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\left\{u\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\ |\ \mathrm{neigh}(u)\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\right\}\ .

(241)

6.4
Stabilizers of the cluster state

In this section, we consider
certain products of the stabilizer generators
\{G_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{C}}
of the cluster state
W\ket{0^\cC}
defined by (
194
).
The first set of operators will be used to define an error syndrome computable from single-qubit measurements performed in our protocol. To introduce
these operators, we denote by
\mathrm{Inci}_{G}(v)
the set of all edges of a graph
G
which are incident on a vertex
v
.
Here the graph
G
is either
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
or
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
.
Again using the labeling map
\Lambda
(cf. (
228
)), we will sometimes regard
\mathrm{Inci}_{G}(v)
as a set of sites (associated with the measured qubits).
Key to our procedure is the fact that certain products of the stabilizer generators (
194
) of the cluster state
W\ket{0^\cC}
have supports given by sets of the form
\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(v)
for
v\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
or
\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(v)
for
v\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
. More precisely, we have the following:

Lemma 6.1
.

Define
S^{u}
for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
as

\displaystyle S^{u}:=\begin{cases}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}G_{v}\qquad&\text{if $u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}$}\\
G_{u}&\text{if $u_{3}\in\{1,R\}$}\ .\end{cases}

(242)

Then

\displaystyle S^{u}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{A}}Z_{v}\right)\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}}X_{v}\right)\ .

(243)

Similarly, define
S^{u}
for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
as

\displaystyle S^{u}=\begin{cases}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}G_{v}\qquad&{ if $u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}$}\\
G_{u\pm(0,0,1)}\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}}G_{v}\qquad&{ if $u_{3}\in\{1,R\}$}\end{cases}

(244)

where the sign
\pm
in (
244
) is “
+
” if
u
is situated in the front layer, and “
-
” if
u
is situated in the back layer so that
u\pm(0,0,1)\in\mathcal{C}
.
Then

\displaystyle S^{u}=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(u)}Z_{v}\qquad\textrm{ for all }\qquad u\in\ \mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}\ .

(245)

We include a proof of this lemma in Appendix
A
.

We also need two additional products
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
of the stabilizer generators
\{G_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{C}}
. These have support on the two qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}

between which the protocol establishes entanglement, and their eigenvalues fix the state of these two qubits as described below (see Lemma
6.3
).

Lemma 6.2
.

Define the operators

\displaystyle S^{X}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{u\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}G_{u}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad S^{Z}:=\prod_{u\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cup\left\{q_{1},q_{2}\right\}}G_{u}\ .

(246)

Then

\displaystyle S^{X}

\displaystyle=X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}X\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}\right)Z\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)

(247)

\displaystyle S^{Z}

\displaystyle=Z_{q_{1}}Z_{q_{2}}Z\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}\right)Z\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}\right)\ .

(248)

We note that similar expressions for products of stabilizer generators have been exploited in the seminal work
[
48
]
. For completeness, we give a proof of this lemma in Appendix
A
.

6.5
Description of the entanglement generation protocol

The entanglement generation protocol is given as Algorithm
2
below. It takes as input a state
\ket{\Psi_{\textrm{in}}}
of the qubits on
\mathcal{C}
and produces a two-qubit state
\ket{\Psi_{\textrm{out}}}
on qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
.

In the following, we again identify a string
a\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{C}}
with a subset of
\mathcal{C}
(interpreting
a
as its characteristic function). The bit-wise XOR
a\oplus b
of two strings
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{C}}
then corresponds to taking the symmetric difference of the associated sets.

Algorithm 2

Fault-tolerant entanglement generation protocol

1:

Input: A state on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{C}}
, where
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]
is the set of the locations of qubits.

2:

Perform the following measurements: measure

every qubit in the set

in the

denote the outcome by

\mathcal{X}

X
-basis

x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}

\mathcal{Z}

Z
-basis

z\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{Z}}

\mathcal{A}

Z
-basis

a\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{A}}

3:

Compute the boundaries of the edges
(a\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})\oplus x
and
(a\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}})\oplus z
, respectively, i.e., set

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\leftarrow\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left((a\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})\oplus x\right)

(249)

\displaystyle s^{*}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\left((a\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}})\oplus z\right)

(250)

4:

Compute minimal matchings

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(s)

(251)

\displaystyle m^{*}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(s^{*})\

(252)

5:

Compute the bits

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{X}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{m},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(253)

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{Z}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{m^{*}},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\

(254)

6:

Determine the bits

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{X}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{X}

(255)

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{Z}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{Z}

(256)

7:

Apply
Z^{\hat{c}_{X}}X^{\hat{c}_{Z}}
to
q_{1}
.

8:

Return the two-qubit state on the qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
.

We will show that if the input state
\ket{\Psi_{\textrm{in}}}
is the cluster state
W\ket{0^\cC}
, then the output of Algorithm
2
is the two-qubit Bell state
\ket{\Phi}
.
More generally, we will show that the procedure yields a Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
if we start with a cluster state corrupted by a Pauli error.

6.6
Entanglement generation from a corrupted cluster state

In this section, we examine the result of running Algorithm
2
on the corrupted cluster state

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi^{\cC}_{\textrm{in}}}

\displaystyle=EW\ket{0^\cC}\

(257)

for a fixed Pauli error
E
.
We show that the final state on qubits
q_{1},q_{2}
is one of the four Bell states
\{\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}\}_{\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}}
, and give a formula for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{0,1\}^{2}
in terms of the error
E
(see Theorem
6.6
). This is the basis of our analysis of local stochastic noise in Section
7
.

To determine the state at the end of the protocol, first consider the post-measurement state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(a,x,z,E)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p(a,x,z|E)}}\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}}\right)EW\ket{0^{\cC}}\

(258)

obtained after step
2
of the algorithm on qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
. Here
p(a,x,z\mid E)
denotes the probability of obtaining the outcomes
(a,x,z)
given a Pauli error
E
, i.e., for the input state (
257
). This state is a Bell state determined by the measurement outcomes
(a,x,z)
and the error
E
as follows:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
be the operators introduced in Lemma
6.2
. Let

\displaystyle c_{X}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

(259)

\displaystyle c_{Z}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(260)

Then, given measurement outcomes
(a,x,z)
,
the post-measurement state after Step
2
of Algorithm
2
running on a corrupted input state
EW\ket{0^{\cC}}
is

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(a,x,z,E)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\Phi_{(c_X,c_Z)}}\ .

(261)

Proof.

The claim is an immediate consequence of the following two facts:

(i)

The operators
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}

are defined as products of the stabilizer generators
\{G_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{C}}
and thus stabilize the ideal cluster state
W\ket{0^\cC}
.

(ii)

The operators
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
are – up to additional Pauli operators on the measured qubits – proportional to the stabilizer generators
\{X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}},Z_{q_{1}}Z_{q_{2}}\}
of the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(0,0)}}
, see (
247
) and (
248
) of Lemma
6.2
.

Replacing
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
in (
258
) by
S^{X}W\ket{0^{\cC}}
using property (
i
), we observe that the post-measurement state
\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}=\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}(a,x,z,E)

satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{p}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}
