\displaystyle=\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}}\right)ES^{X}E\ket{0^{\cC}}

(262)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}}\right)S^{X}E\ket{0^{\cC}}

(263)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}\right)EW\ket{0^{\cC}}\ ,

(264)

where
p:=p(a,x,z|E)
and where we used (
247
) as well as the identities

\displaystyle X(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B})H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}_{\mathcal{X}}

(265)

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A})\ket{a}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}\ket{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\ .

(266)

We conclude that

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)}X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

(267)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{c_{X}}X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}\ ,

(268)

by definition of
c_{X}
.

In an analogous manner, we can check
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}=(-1)^{c_{Z}}Z_{q_{1}}Z_{q_{2}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}
by replacing
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
in (
258
) by
S^{Z}W\ket{0^{\cC}}
. Thus
\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}

is stabilized by
\{(-1)^{c_{X}}X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}},(-1)^{c_{Z}}Z_{q_{1}}Z_{q_{2}}\}
. This implies the claim.
∎

According to Lemma
6.3
, the post-measurement state is fully determined by the single-qubit measurement results
(a,x,z)
and the bits
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)
and
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
. These bits in turn are
determined by the physical errors on qubits
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
as well as a certain restriction of the error as specified in the following statement:

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
E
be a Pauli operator on
\mathcal{C}
and let
S^{X}
and
S^{Z}
be the Pauli operators defined in Lemma
6.2
.
Let us decompose
E
into a product
E=E^{X}E^{Z}
of Pauli-
X
- and Pauli-
Z
-operators, respectively. Define

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}}E^{Z}|_{\mathcal{X}}\qquad\textrm{ and }

(269)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}

\displaystyle=E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\

(270)

where
F|_{\Omega}:=\prod_{j\in\Omega}F_{j}
for any Pauli operator
F=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{C}}F_{j}
on
\mathcal{C}
and
\Omega\subset\mathcal{C}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(271)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(272)

Proof.

Let
E
be an arbitrary Pauli operator on
\mathcal{C}
.
Using expression (
247
) for
S^{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}\left(X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}X\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}\right)Z\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}\right),E\right)

(273)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(274)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z}|_{\mathcal{X}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}|_{{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(275)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(276)

by definition of
E_{\mathrm{gl}}
, see Eq. (
269
).
A similar calculation yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}|_{\mathcal{Z}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\mathrm{supp}\left(E^{X}|_{{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}}\right)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(277)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ ,

(278)

as claimed.
∎

Unfortunately, the operators
E_{\mathrm{gl}}
and
E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}
defined in (
269
) and (
270
) are not determined by the measurement results
(a,x,z)
. In particular,
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)
and
\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)
cannot be computed from the measurement outcomes. Algorithm
2
therefore generates estimates
\hat{s}^{X}
and
\hat{s}^{Z}
for these bits, see step (
5
).

To motivate the definition of these bits, and compute the final state after the entanglement generation protocol, we discuss how the measurement results
(a,x,z)
constrain the error
E
, or more precisely
the operators
E_{\mathrm{gl}}
and
E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}
. Observe that by their definitions (
269
) and (
270
), these operators are fully determined by the supports
\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})
and
\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})
.
We obtain constraints on these sets expressed in terms of the strings
s,s^{*}
computed in step
3
of Algorithm
2
.

Lemma 6.5
.

The subsets
s\subset\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
s^{*}\subset\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
computed (from the measurement outcomes
(a,x,z)
) in
Step
3
satisfy

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}))

(279)

\displaystyle s^{*}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}))\ .

(280)

Proof.

Let
s=(s_{u})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}
be the string computed in step
3
, i.e.,

\displaystyle s=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left({a\cap\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\oplus x\right)\ ,

(281)

and let
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
. Linearity of the map
\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}
implies that

\displaystyle s_{u}

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left(a\right)\right)_{u}\oplus\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left(x\right)\right)_{u}

(282)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(283)

Since

\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})\right)\right)_{u}=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle
, we need to show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(284)

We first show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)\ .

(285)

Note that
S^{u}
stabilizes the state
W\ket{0^\cC}
since it is a product of the stabilizer generators
\{G_{v}\}_{v}
of
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
.
Replacing
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
in (
258
) by
S^{u}W\ket{0^{\cC}}
yields

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}}\right)ES^{u}W\ket{0^{\cC}}

(286)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)}\left(\bra{a}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\bra{x}_{\mathcal{X}}H(\mathcal{X})^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{z}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes I_{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}}\right)S^{u}EW\ket{0^{\cC}}

(287)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{a},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{pm}}}\ .

(288)

Here the third equality follows from the identity

\displaystyle S^{u}\left(\ket{a}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{a},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-2.1097pt\left\langle{x},{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}\right\rangle\hskip-2.1097pt\right\rangle}\left(\ket{a}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathcal{X})\ket{x}\right)\

(289)

which is a consequence of (
243
) and the fact that
a\subset\mathcal{A}
and
x\subset\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{B}
are disjoint.
Identity (
288
) together with (
283
) establishes (
285
).

Next, we show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)},{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)\ .

(290)

Observe that according to (
243
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Syn}(S^{u},E)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}\left(\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{A}}Z_{v}\right)\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}}X_{v}\right),E\right)

(291)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{A}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(292)

for any
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.
From this expression, the definition (
269
)
of
E_{\mathrm{gl}}
and the fact that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are disjoint, the identity (
290
) follows.
Together (
285
), the claim (
284
) follows.

The claim for
s^{*}
can be proved in an analogous manner using (
245
).
∎

We now argue that the strings
m
and
m^{*}
computed
in Step
4

of Algorithm
2
can be considered to be proxys for the sets
\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})
and
\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})
. More precisely, we may define
a Pauli error

\displaystyle\widehat{E}:=\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}\qquad\textrm{ with }\qquad\begin{matrix}\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}&:=&X(m\cap\mathcal{A})Z(m\cap\mathcal{B})\\
\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}&:=&Z(m^{*})\ .\end{matrix}

(293)

Then we have
m=\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}})
and
m^{*}=\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})
by definition of these operators, and it follows immediately from the
definition of
m
and
m^{*}

that
(\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}},\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})

satisfy constraints analogous to the constraints (
279
), (
280
) obeyed by
(E_{\mathrm{gl}},E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}))

(294)

\displaystyle s^{*}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}))\ .

(295)

Thus
\widehat{E}=\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}
is an error consistent with the observed syndrome
(s,s^{*})
with the property that

\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}
and
\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}

each are of minimum weight relative to
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
, respectively.

Because the error
\widehat{E}
can be computed from the measurement outcomes, this motivates using the pair
\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},\widehat{E}),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},\widehat{E})\right)
as an estimate for

\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\right)
. Indeed, this is the reasoning underlying the definition

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\hat{s}^{X}&:=&\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}},{m}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\\
\hat{s}^{Z}&:=&\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}},{m^{*}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\end{matrix}

(296)

in Step
5
of the algorithm: It is easy to check that
(\hat{s}^{X},\hat{s}^{Z})
defined in this way satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{X}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}})

(297)

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{Z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\

(298)

because of expressions (
271
) and since
\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}
and
\widehat{E}_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}
have no support on
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
.

Having motivated the algorithm, let us next give an expression for the resulting final state when the algorithm is run on a corrupted cluster state.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
E
be a Pauli error on
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose we run Algorithm
2
on the input state
EW\ket{0^\cC}
.
Then the output is the Bell state
\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}
where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}))\right)},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(299)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\right)},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(300)

Proof.

Because
m,m^{*}
are equal to

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(s)=\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}))\right)

(301)

\displaystyle m^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(s^{*})=\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\right)\ ,

(302)

see Step
4
of the algorithm and Lemma
6.5
, the claim amounts to the statement that the output state is the Bell state
\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}
where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})\oplus m},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

(303)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\oplus m^{*}},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(304)

According to Lemma
6.3
, the
post-measurement state
after Step
2

of Algorithm
2
is the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(c_X,c_Z)}}

with

\displaystyle c_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

(305)

\displaystyle c_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(306)

Since the algorithm applies
the correction operation
Z^{\hat{c}_{X}}X^{\hat{c}_{Z}}

in Step
7
, the output of the algorithm is the Bell state
\ket{\Psi_{(c_X\oplus\hat{c}_X,c_Z\oplus\hat{c}_Z)}}
. It follows with the definition of
(\hat{c}_{X},\hat{c}_{Z})
in Step
6
,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{x},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{X}

(307)

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{a},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{A}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{z},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\cap\mathcal{B}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\hat{s}^{Z}

(308)

that
the output is the Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\hat{s}^{X}\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E)

(309)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\hat{s}^{Z}\oplus\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\ .

(310)

The claim now follows by combining the expressions (
271
), (
272
) for
\left(\mathsf{Syn}(S^{X},E),\mathsf{Syn}(S^{Z},E)\right)
from Lemma
6.4
with the
Definitions (
296
) of
(\hat{s}^{X},\hat{s}^{Z})
used in Step (
5
) of the algorithm.
∎

We note that all theorems and lemmas in this section still hold if we replace the function
\mathsf{MinMatch}
on
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with any function on
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
with binary output.
The fact that
\mathsf{MinMatch}
produces a matching is – up to this point – only used to motivate the definition of
\hat{s}^{X}
and
\hat{s}^{Z}
in Algorithm
2
.
We will use this property of
\mathsf{MinMatch}
in Section
7
to show that our protocol is robust against local stochastic noise.

7
Entanglement generation with local stochastic noise

In this section, we show that the entanglement generation protocol given by Algorithm
2
applied to the cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
is robust against local stochastic noise
E
of any noise strength
p
below some threshold
p_{0}
. In more detail, we consider a cluster state lattice
\mathcal{C}
of the form
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]
(with
d=O(\log R)
) and show that
even when the ideal cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
is replaced by
EW\ket{0^{\cC}}
, the resulting state has fidelity at least
1-O(p)
with the Bell state
\Phi
independently of
R
(see Corollary
7.4
). In particular, the range
R
of the entanglement generation can be exponential in the surface code distance
d
while still generating constant-fidelity entanglement.

We proceed as follows: In Section
7.1
, we construct upper bounds on the resilience functions
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)
and
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}(p)
of the recovery sets
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}
. In Section
7.2
, we then establish a lower bound on the success probability of the entanglement generation protocol using the results of Section
7.1
.

7.1
Bounds on the resilience function for
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}

Here we establish upper bounds on the resilience functions of the recovery sets
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}

for the decoding graphs
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
for small values of
p
. These upper bounds immediately give lower bounds on the success probability of the entanglement generation protocol, see Section
7.2
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Consider the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
with set of internal vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
defined on the lattice
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}[d\times d\times R]
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
be the recovery set introduced in Section
6.3.2
. Then the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)\leq\left(2402+100d(R-1)\left(10\sqrt{p}\right)^{d-2}\right)\cdot p\

(311)

for any
p\in\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{400}\mathclose{]}
.

Proof.

Let
L\in Z_{\circ}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
be a simple closed loop. Then it is clear from the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
that
L
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
have no overlap, and in particular,
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{L},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=0
. This implies that simple closed loops do not contribute to the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)
(see Definition (
72
)).

To describe the set
Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
of simple paths connecting external vertices through internal vertices,
we partition the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}=\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cup\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
of external vertices into the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
of vertices forming the “left” boundary of the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

and the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
of remaining external vertices forming the “right”, “front”, and “back” boundaries.
We label the external vertices in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

\displaystyle=\left\{u_{j}^{k}\mid 1\leq j\leq d-1,k\in\left\{0,\frac{R+1}{2}\right\}\right\}\cup\left\{u_{j}^{k}\mid 0\leq j\leq d-1,1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}\right\}

(312)

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

\displaystyle=\left\{v_{i,j}^{k}\mid 1\leq i\leq d-1,0\leq j\leq i,k\in\left\{0,\frac{R+1}{2}\right\}\right\}\cup\left\{v_{j}^{k}\mid 0\leq j\leq d-1,1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}\right\}\ ,

(313)

see Fig.
10
.
Written out, the external vertices are

\displaystyle u_{j}^{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(0,2j,1)&{ for $k=0$, $1\leq j\leq d-1$}\\
(0,2j,2k)&{ for $1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}$, $0\leq j\leq d-1$}\\
(0,2j,R)&{ for $k=\frac{R+1}{2}$, $1\leq j\leq d-1$}\end{cases}

(314)

\displaystyle v_{i,j}^{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(2i,2j,1)&{ for $k=0$, $1\leq i\leq d-1$, $0\leq j\leq i$}\\
(2i,2j,R)&{ for $k=\frac{R+1}{2}$, $1\leq i\leq d-1$, $0\leq j\leq i$}\end{cases}

(315)

\displaystyle v_{j}^{k}

\displaystyle=(2d,2j,2k)\qquad\textrm{ for $1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}$, $0\leq j\leq d-1$}\ .

(316)

It is clear from the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
that a simple path
P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
satisfies
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{P},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1
if and only if
P
connects an external vertex
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
to another external vertex
v\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.
Here we say such a path
P
(through internal vertices) starts at
u
and ends at
v
.
Let us denote the set of all simple paths of length
\ell
which start at
u_{j}^{k}\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
and end at
v\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
through internal vertices
by
\Delta(u_{j}^{k},\ell)
.
Then we can write the set of all simple paths of length
\ell
in
Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})
satisfying
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{P},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1
as

\displaystyle\{P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}})\mid\absolutevalue{P}=\ell\textrm{ and }\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{P},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\Delta(u,\ell)\ .

(317)

Note that
\Delta(u,\ell)
and
\Delta(u^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
are disjoint for
(u,\ell)\neq(u^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})

since the corresponding paths either start at different vertices or have different lengths.
We also note that
\Delta(u,\ell)
is empty for sufficiently large
\ell
since we are considering simple paths and the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
is finite.
It follows from the definition (
72
) of the resilience function and from (
317
) that

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\binom{\ell}{\lceil{\ell/u}\rceil}\cdot\absolutevalue{\bigcup_{u \in\Vcldecextleft} \Delta(u, \ell)}\cdot p^{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}

(318)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\sum_{\ell=L_{u}^{\min}}^{L_{u}^{\max}}\binom{\ell}{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\cdot\absolutevalue{\Delta(u, \ell)}\cdot p^{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\ .

(319)

Here
L_{u}^{\min}
and
L_{u}^{\max}
are the minimal and maximal lengths of paths in
\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\Delta(u,\ell)
for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.

We rewrite the expression (
319
) by partitioning the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
as follows.
Let us define

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(s)=\left\{u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\mid L_{u}^{\min}=s\right\}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad s\geq 1\ .

(320)

In other words,
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(s)
is the set of external vertices
u
in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
such that the minimal distance to
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
is
s
in terms of the length of paths connecting
u
and
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.
It is clear from the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
that the set
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(s)
is empty for all
s>d
.
Furthermore, the sets
\{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(s)\}_{s=1}^{d}
are pairwise disjoint and their union is
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
.
Thus we can write the expression (
319
) as

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)=\sum_{s=1}^{d}A_{s}\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad A_{s}=\sum_{u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(s)}\sum_{\ell=s}^{L_{u}^{\max}}\binom{\ell}{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\cdot\absolutevalue{\Delta(u, \ell)}\cdot p^{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\ .

(321)

We first compute
A_{1}
.
It is clear from the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
that
u_{1}^{0}
and
u_{1}^{\frac{R+1}{2}}
are the only vertices in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(1)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(1)

\displaystyle=\left\{u_{1}^{0},u_{1}^{\frac{R+1}{2}}\right\}\ .

(322)

In addition, the set of all simple paths through internal vertices starting at either
u_{1}^{0}
or
u_{1}^{\frac{R+1}{1}}
and ending at any external vertex in
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{right}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
consists of only two length-
1
paths, the path
\left(\left\{u_{1}^{0},v_{1,1}^{0}\right\}\right)
and the path
\left(\left\{u_{1}^{\frac{R+1}{2}},v_{1,1}^{\frac{R+1}{2}}\right\}\right)
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle L_{u_{1}^{0}}^{\max}=L_{u_{1}^{\frac{R+1}{2}}}^{\max}=1\ .

(323)

According to (
322
) and (
323
) with the definition of
A_{1}
from (
321
), we have

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\binom{1}{\lceil{1/2}\rceil}\cdot\absolutevalue{\Delta(u_1^{0}, 1)}\cdot p^{\lceil{1/2}\rceil}+\binom{1}{\lceil{1/2}\rceil}\cdot\absolutevalue{\Delta(u_1^{\frac{R+1}{2}}, 1)}\cdot p^{\lceil{1/2}\rceil}

(324)

\displaystyle=2\cdot p\ .

(325)

Next, we consider
A_{d}
.
It is clear from the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
that
u_{j}^{k}\not\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl,dec}}(d)
for
1\leq j\leq d-1
and
k\in\left\{0,\frac{R+1}{2}\right\}
.
This gives the upper bound

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Vextleft{d}}

\displaystyle\leq\absolutevalue{\Vcldecextleft\setminus\left\{u_j^{k} \mid 1 \leq j \leq d-1, k \in\left\{0,\frac{R+1}{2}\right\}\right\}}

(326)

\displaystyle=d\cdot\left(\frac{R-1}{2}\right)\ .

(327)

The fact that the decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
has vertices of degree at most 6 implies that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta(u, \ell)}\leq 5^{\ell}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext},\mathrm{left}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\ .

(328)

Combining (
326
), (
328
) and the fact that
\binom{\ell}{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\leq 2^{\ell}
and
p^{\lceil{\ell/2}\rceil}\leq p^{\ell/2}
with the definition (
321
) of
A_{s}
with
s=d
, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{d}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot\left(\frac{R-1}{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=d}^{\infty}2^{\ell}\cdot 5^{\ell}\cdot p^{\ell/2}

(329)

\displaystyle=d\cdot\left(\frac{R-1}{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=d}^{\infty}(10\sqrt{p})^{\ell}

(330)

\displaystyle=d\cdot\left(\frac{R-1}{2}\right)\frac{q^{d}}{1-q}\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad q:=10\sqrt{p}\ .

(331)

Here the geometric series converges since the assumption
0\leq p\leq\frac{1}{400}
implies
0\leq q\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

It remains to calculate an upper bound of
\sum_{s=2}^{d-1}A_{s}
.
Straightforward counting yields

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Vextleft{s}}\leq 2\cdot s\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad 2\leq s\leq d-1\ .

(332)

It follows from an analogous argument as for the upper bound on
A_{d}
that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=2}^{d-1}A_{s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=2}^{d-1}\left(2\cdot s\sum_{\ell=s}^{\infty}2^{\ell}\cdot 5^{\ell}\cdot p^{\ell/2}\right)

(333)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=2}^{d-1}\left(2\cdot s\cdot\frac{q^{s}}{1-q}\right)\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad q:=10\sqrt{p}

(334)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4q^{2}}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{2q^{3}}{(1-q)^{3}}\ .

(335)

Here again, the geometric- and the arithmetico-geometric series converge since
0\leq q\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Combining (
324
), (
329
) and (
333
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{d}A_{s}

(336)

\displaystyle\leq 2p+\frac{4q^{2}}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{2q^{3}}{(1-q)^{3}}+d\cdot\frac{R-1}{2}\cdot\frac{q^{d}}{1-q}

(337)

\displaystyle=\left[2+\frac{400}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{200q}{(1-q)^{3}}+50d(R-1)\cdot\frac{(10\sqrt{p})^{d-2}}{1-q}\right]\cdot p\ .

(338)

Here we used the definition of
q
for the last equality.
We have
q\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{1-q}\leq 2
, hence we obtain the desired bound (
311
) from (
338
).
∎

The resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}(p)
of the dual decoding graph and the recovery set
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}
can be upper bounded in a similar manner.

Lemma 7.2
.

Consider the dual decoding graph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}=(\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}})
with set of internal vertices
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
defined on the dual lattice
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}[d\times d\times R]
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
be the recovery set introduced in Section
6.3.2
. Then the resilience function
\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}(p)

\displaystyle\leq\left[2400+100d(R+1)(10\sqrt{p})^{d-2}\right]\cdot p\ .

(339)

for any
p\in\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{400}\mathclose{]}
.

The proof mirrors that of Lemma
7.1

and is given in Appendix
C
for completeness.

7.2
A lower bound on the success probability of the protocol

Now we state our main result, a lower bound on the success probability of our entanglement generation protocol. Here the success probability (denoted
\nu_{(0,0)}
in the following) is the probability (taken over the random choice of local stochastic error
E
as well as the measurements outcomes) that the final state of the protocol is the Bell state
\Phi=\Phi_{(0,0)}
. Note that this quantity is in one-to-one-correspondence with the fidelity of the (average) output state with the Bell state
\Phi
. This is because the protocol always produces one of the four orthogonal Bell states.

Theorem 7.3

(Entanglement generation with local stochastic noise)
.

Let
d\geq 2
, and let
R\geq 3
be an odd integer.
Suppose we run Algorithm
2
on a noisy cluster state
EW\ket{0^{\cC}}
on a lattice
\mathcal{C}\left[d\times d\times R\right]
, where the cluster state is corrupted by a local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
with
p\in\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{400}\mathclose{]}
.
Then there is a probability distribution
\nu=(\nu_{(0,0)},\nu_{(0,1)},\nu_{(1,0)},\nu_{(1,1)})
such that the following holds:

(i)

The resulting output state is the two-qubit Bell state
\ket{\Phi_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
with probability
\nu_{(\alpha,\beta)}
for every pair
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{0,1\}^{2}
.

(ii)

The success probability
\nu_{(0,0)}
of the entanglement generation protocol satisfies

\displaystyle\nu_{(0,0)}\geq 1-\left[4806+200dR(10\sqrt{p})^{d-2}\right]\cdot p\ .

(340)

Proof.

Due to Lemma
6.3
, it suffices to establish an upper bound on
1-\nu_{(0,0)}
.
Let us introduce the following four random variables:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{Z}&:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\\
B_{Z}&:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\\
A_{X}&:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1},q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\\
B_{X}&:=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}))},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .\end{aligned}

(341)

Then Theorem
6.6
translates to

\displaystyle\nu_{(0,0)}=\Pr[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\textrm{ and }(A_{X},B_{X})\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}]\ .

(342)

The union bound gives

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{(0,0)}\leq\Pr[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}]+\Pr[(A_{X},B_{X})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}]\ .

(343)

Using the union bound on the first term of (
343
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr[(A_{Z},B_{Z})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[(A_{Z},B_{Z})=(0,1)]+\Pr[(A_{Z},B_{Z})=(1,0)]

(344)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[B_{Z}=1]+\Pr[A_{Z}=1]\ .

(345)

Applying the same reasoning to the second term of (
343
) yields

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{(0,0)}\leq\Pr[A_{X}=1]+\Pr[A_{Z}=1]+\Pr[B_{X}=1]+\Pr[B_{Z}=1]\ .

(346)

By the definition of
A_{X}
and (bi-)linearity of
\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle
together with the union bound, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[A_{X}=1]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1]

(347)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{1}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1]+\Pr[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\{q_{2}\}},{\mathrm{supp}(E^{Z})}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1]

(348)

\displaystyle\leq p+p=2p

(349)

where we used that
E^{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
is local stochastic noise with the same parameter as
E
. In a similar manner, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr[A_{Z}=1]=2p\ .

(350)

We also have

\displaystyle\Pr[B_{Z}=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1]

(351)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}(p)

(352)

\displaystyle\leq\left[2402+100d(R-1)(10\sqrt{p})^{d-2}\right]\cdot p\

(353)

where the definition of
B_{Z}
, the fact that
E_{\mathrm{gl}}\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
, Proposition
4.3
and Lemma
7.1
are used.
By similar reasoning, we get

\displaystyle\Pr[B_{X}=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr[\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}^{*}}}\mathrm{supp}(E_{\mathrm{gl}}^{*}))},{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle]

(354)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathrm{cl},Z}}(p)

(355)

\displaystyle\leq\left[2400+100d(R+1)(10\sqrt{p})^{d-2}\right]\cdot p\ ,

(356)

see Lemma
7.2
.
Combining inequalities (
349
), (
350
), (
353
) and (
356
) together with (
346
), we obtain the claim (
340
).
∎

Theorem
7.3
immediately implies the following corollary. It shows that for any sufficiently small constant noise-strength, a constant-fidelity Bell state can be established over a distance
R
which is exponential in
d
.

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
p\in\mathopen{(}0,\frac{1}{5006}\mathclose{]}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq 3
and
R\in\mathbb{N}
be odd with
R\geq 3
.
Suppose we run Algorithm
2
on the noisy cluster state
EW\ket{0^\cC}
with local stochastic noise
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
in the lattice
\mathcal{C}\left[d\times d\times R\right]
with

\displaystyle R\leq\frac{1}{d}\left(\frac{1}{10\sqrt{p}}\right)^{d-2}\ .

(357)

Then the probability
\nu_{(0,0)}
of successful entanglement generation satisfies

\displaystyle\nu_{(0,0)}\geq 1-5006p\ .

(358)

Proof.

Due to the condition
p\in\mathopen{(}0,\frac{1}{5006}\mathclose{]}\subset\mathopen{[}0,\frac{1}{400}\mathclose{]}
, the inequality (
340
) holds. Assuming that
R
satisfies the upper bound (
357
), the claim follows.
∎

8
Limits on low-latency entanglement generation

Here we establish limits on fault-tolerant long-range entanglement generation using adaptive shallow (i.e., constant-depth) circuits
along a line of repeater stations. Such circuits capture low-latency schemes where the entanglement is generated in a constant amount of time (up to local corrections).
We show that any such scheme which is resilient to arbitrary local stochastic noise of strength below some threshold can only establish constant-fidelity entanglement up to a certain distance
R
: We show that
R
must be upper bounded by a function which is exponential in the number
m
of qubits at each repeater station.

In Section
8.1
, we establish a general converse bound for low-latency entanglement generation, applicable to all protocols realized by constant-depth adaptive circuits.
In Section
8.2
, we establish a converse bound for any scheme based on the cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
which uses the same measurement pattern and syndrome information as Algorithm
2
, but possibly different classical decoding strategies. We discuss how these two converse bounds compare to each other and to our achievability results for Algorithm
2
from Section
7.2
.

8.1
A general converse bound for low-latency entanglement generation

In the following, we consider constant-depth protocols for entanglement generation across a line of
R
site with
m
qubits per site (i.e., per repeater). Constant-depth here means that the entanglement generation protocol can be written as a circuit of depth independent of
(m,R)
composed of single-qubit gates and measurements, and two-qubit gates between any pair of qubits located at the same or two neighboring repeaters. Throughout, we consider adaptive circuits, i.e., circuits that apply unitaries which are classically controlled by (functions of) measurement results. Classical computations (i.e., function evaluations) to determine the unitaries which are applied are not counted in the definition of circuit depth. Furthermore, for our converse bound, it actually suffices to consider the circuit depth “between repeaters”, i.e., gate sequences within a repeater station can be assumed to contribute only a single gate layer to the overall circuit depth
\Delta
.

We assume that the considered protocols generate entanglement between a qubit
q_{1}
at the first repeater (
u_{3}=1
) and a qubit
q_{2}
at the last repeater (
u_{3}=R
). The main result of this section is the following converse. It shows that long-range entanglement cannot be generated fault-tolerantly by constant-depth adaptive circuits if the distance is more than exponential in the number
m
of qubits at each repeater.

Corollary 8.1
.

Let
\pi
be a depth-
\Delta
circuit for entanglement generation between two qubits at distance
R
using
m
qubits per repeater.
Suppose there is some constant threshold
p_{0}<1
such that
\pi
produces the Bell state
\Phi
with probability at least
1/e\approx 0.368
for any local stochastic noise of strength
p\leq p_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{3}{4p_{0}^{2}}\right)^{\Delta m}\ .

(359)

Corollary
8.1
is an immediate consequence of the following general no-go result for entanglement generation with limited-depth quantum circuits:

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\pi
be a circuit of depth
\Delta
for distance-
R
entanglement generation using
m
qubits per repeater.
Let
p\in(0,1)
be arbitrary. Then there is a local stochastic noise model of strength
p

acting on the circuit such that the probability that

\pi
produces a separable state is lower bounded by

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{fail}}\geq 1-e^{-R(4p^{2}/3)^{\Delta m}}\ .

(360)

Reformulated, Theorem
8.2
implies that any protocol
\pi
that can withstand arbitrary local stochastic noise of strength
p
on the initial state and still succeeds (i.e., produces the Bell pair
\Phi
) with probability
p_{\mathrm{succ}}
must satisfy the relationship

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\leq({{3}/{(4p^{2})}})^{\Delta m}\log\left(1/p_{\mathrm{succ}}\right)\ .

(361)

In particular, any protocol that produces constant-fidelity entanglement for any error strength below some threshold can at most reach a distance exponential in
m
.

The proof of Theorem
8.2

proceeds by an intermediate step, where we consider bipartite-entanglement-based protocols. To describe this in detail, let
\pi
be a distance-
R
-entanglement generation protocol using
m
qubits per repeater which can be implemented by a circuit of depth
\Delta
.

We assume without loss of generality that the only two-qubit gates that
\pi
uses between neighboring sites are
\mathsf{CNOT}
-gates.
Note that each two-qubit unitary can be realized by a simple circuit consisting of at most three
\mathsf{CNOT}
gates and (arbitrary) single-qubit gates
[
52
]
.
Each such
\mathsf{CNOT}
-gate can be realized by a gate-teleportation based circuit as shown in Fig.
11
. This circuit consumes a shared resource Bell state
\Phi
.
We note that the
\mathsf{CNOT}
-implementing circuit uses only local gates (where locality is with respect to the bipartition, i.e., we allow two-qubit gates at the “source” and “target” sites), local measurements, and classical communication. In particular, this shows that the
\mathsf{CNOT}
gate can be implemented using a Bell state
\Phi
and LOCC-operations only.

Applying this substitution to every
\mathsf{CNOT}
-gate of
\pi
which acts between neighboring repeaters, we obtain a bipartite-entanglement-based protocol
\pi^{\prime}
which has the following properties:

(i)

The protocol
\pi^{\prime}
uses a resource state
(\Phi^{\otimes t})_{A^{t}B^{t}}
with
t\leq\Delta m
copies of
\Phi
shared between any neighboring pair
(S_{j},S_{j+1})
of sites. Here
t
is the maximal number of two-qubit
\mathsf{CNOT}
gates executed between two neighboring sites.

(ii)

The protocol
\pi^{\prime}
is an LOCC-operation between
S_{1}:S_{2}:\cdots:S_{R}
.

(iii)

Local stochastic noise on
\pi^{\prime}
is equivalent to local stochastic noise on
\pi
. This follows from the fact that the substitution rule in Fig.
11
is local by suitably commuting errors as in the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 11]
. In fact, we only need the following special version: A local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
of strength
p
acting on
the
t
qubits
A^{t}=A_{1}\cdots A_{t}
(i.e., one half of the Bell states) in the resource state
(\Phi^{\otimes t})_{A^{t}B^{t}}
followed by an error-free execution of
\pi^{\prime}

is equivalent to executing
\pi
with local stochastic noise of strengh
\sqrt{p}
. This is because
in the circuit of Fig.
11
,
a single-qubit
Y
error on qubit
A^{\prime}
is equivalent to a two-qubit error
Z_{A}X_{B}
after the
\mathsf{CNOT}
, whereas single-qubit
X
- and
Z
-errors on
A^{\prime}
become single-qubit errors
X_{B}
and
Z_{A}
, respectively.

A

A

\Phi_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

A

Z

Z

A^{\prime}

B^{\prime}

H

H

B

X

X

Figure 11
:
Gate-teleportation
[
24
]
based protocol for realizing a two-qubit
\mathsf{CNOT}_{AB}
gate between two sites, see also
[
15
,
46
]
. The circuit using a Bell state
\Phi_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
as a resource, as well as local operations and measurements.

The following lemma gives an upper bound on such an entanglement-based protocol
\pi^{\prime}
for long-range entanglement generation.

Lemma 8.3
.

Consider
R
sites
S_{1},\ldots,S_{R}
of the form

\displaystyle S_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}A_{1}C_{1}\qquad&\textrm{ for }j=1\\
A_{j}B_{j}C_{j}&\textrm{ for }1<j<R\\
B_{R}C_{R}&\textrm{ for }j=R\ ,\end{cases}

(362)

where
A_{j}\cong B_{j}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes t}
for each
j
. Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a constant-depth adaptive quantum circuit that acts on
S_{1}\cdots S_{R}
and whose output state is on
C_{1}C_{R}
.
Suppose
\pi^{\prime}
satisfies properties (
i
) and (
ii
).

Assume that
\pi^{\prime}
is an LOCC-operation with respect to the multipartition

\displaystyle S_{1}:S_{2}:\cdots:S_{R}\ .

(363)

Suppose we apply
\pi^{\prime}
to an initial state of the form

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathrm{initial}}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R-1}(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes t})_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R}\rho_{C_{j}}\

(364)

where
\Phi\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the two-qubit Bell state, and
\rho_{C_{1}},\ldots,\rho_{C_{R}}
are arbitrary.
Then, for any
p\in(0,1)
, there is a local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p)
acting on the systems
A^{R}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{R})
of the initial state (
364
)
such that the final state
\rho^{\mathrm{initial}}_{C_{1}C_{R}}
is a separable state (with respect to the bipartition
C_{1}:C_{R}
) with probability at least

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{fail}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-e^{-R\left({{4p}/{3}}\right)^{t}}\ .

(365)

Proof.

This statement immediately follows from the observation that a protocol
\pi^{\prime}
can only convert preexisting entanglement in the initial state
\rho^{\textrm{initial}}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}
to end up being on systems
C_{1}:C_{R}
. Furthermore, the initial state contains bipartite entanglement across sites only between
the pair of systems
A_{j}B_{j+1}
, for
j=1,\ldots,R-1
. If this shared entanglement is lost due to noise then the state on
C_{1}C_{R}
remains separable throughout the execution of the (ideal) protocol.

For concreteness, assume that the depolarizing noise channel

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(1-p)\rho+p\tr(\rho)I/2

(366)

with
0<p\leq 1
is applied to each system
A_{j}
before application of the (ideal) protocol
\pi^{\prime}
.
That is, we consider a noisy initial state of the form

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{initial, noisy}}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R-1}\bigotimes_{k=1}^{t}((\mathcal{E}_{p})_{A_{j}^{(k)}}\otimes\mathsf{id}_{B_{j+1}^{(k)}})(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi}_{A_{j}^{(k)}B^{(k)}_{j+1}})\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R}\rho_{C_{j}}\ .

(367)

For
p\in(0,1)
, we argue that this state can be written as a convex combination

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{initial, noisy}}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}

\displaystyle=p_{0}\rho_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}+\sum_{j=1}^{R-1}p_{j}\rho^{(j)}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{j}:S_{j+1}\cdots S_{R}}

(368)

of a certain state
\rho_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}

and states
\rho^{(j)}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{j}:S_{j+1}\cdots S_{R}}
that are separable across bipartitions of the form
S_{1}\cdots S_{j}:S_{j+1}\cdots S_{R}
, for
j\in[R-1]
where

\displaystyle p_{0}

\displaystyle\leq(1-p^{t})^{R}

(369)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-p^{t}R}\ ,

(370)

where we used the inequality
(1-x)^{r}\leq e^{-xr}
.
In particular, since each reduced state
\rho^{(j)}_{S_{1}S_{R}}
is separable, Eq. (
370
) and the fact that the (ideal) protocol
\pi^{\prime}
is LOCC imply that the generated state is separable with probability at least
1-e^{-p^{t}R}
. The claim follows from this because
the depolarizing channel can be written as
\mathcal{E}_{p}(\rho)=\left(1-\frac{3}{4}p\right)\rho+\frac{p}{4}X\rho X^{\dagger}+\frac{p}{4}Y\rho Y^{\dagger}+\frac{p}{4}Z\rho Z^{\dagger}
, i.e., is equivalent to
applying each Pauli with probability
p/4
.
Thus applying
\mathcal{E}_{p}
to each system
A_{j}
amounts to having a local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(3p/4)
. (Substituting
p
by
4p/3
gives the claim.)

Note that the depolarizing channel
\mathcal{E}_{p}
is entanglement-breaking for any
p\geq 2/3
, see
[
50
]
. Eq. (
370
) is a consequence of the more trivial fact that for any
p\in(0,1)
, the channel
\mathcal{E}_{p}

is a convex combination of the identity channel
\mathrm{id}
and
the depolarizing channel
\mathcal{E}_{1}
which is entanglement-breaking, that is, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{p}(\rho)=(1-p)\ \mathsf{id}(\rho)+p\ \mathcal{E}_{1}(\rho)\ .

(371)

With
p_{0}:=1-p
,
p_{1}:=p
and the convention
\mathcal{E}^{0}=\mathsf{id}
, this implies that for every
j\in[R-1]

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{k=1}^{t}((\mathcal{E}_{p})_{A_{j}^{(k)}}\otimes\mathsf{id}_{B_{j}^{(k)}})(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi}_{A_{j}^{(k)}B^{(k)}_{j+1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\in\{0,1\}}\bigotimes_{k=1}^{t}p_{x_{k}}(\mathcal{E}^{x_{k}}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{id})(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi}_{A_{j}^{(k)}B^{(k)}_{j+1}})

(372)

\displaystyle=\left(p^{t}\left((\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{id})(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi})\right)^{\otimes t}+\left(1-p^{t}\right)\sigma\right)_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}\ ,

(373)

where we collected all terms with
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})\neq 1^{t}

in a state
\sigma_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}
.
Because
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is entanglement-breaking, the state
\rho_{A_{j}:B_{j+1}}:=\left((\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{id})(\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi})\right)^{\otimes t}
is separable across
A_{j}:B_{j+1}
.
We have shown that

\displaystyle\rho^{\textrm{initial, noisy}}_{S_{1}\cdots S_{R}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R-1}((1-q)\sigma_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}+q\rho_{A_{j}:B_{j+1}})\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R}\rho_{C_{j}}\right)

(374)

\displaystyle=(1-q)^{R-1}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R-1}\sigma_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R}\rho_{C_{j}}\right)+\Sigma_{\mathrm{SEP}}

(375)

where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathrm{SEP}}:=\sum_{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{R-1})\in\{0,1\}^{R-1}\backslash\{0^{R-1}\}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R-1}(1-q)^{1-z_{j}}q^{z_{j}}\sigma^{1-z_{j}}_{A_{j}B_{j+1}}\rho_{A_{j}:B_{j+1}}^{z_{j}}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{R}\rho_{C_{j}}\right)\

(376)

and
q:=p^{t}
.
Since every summand in
\Sigma_{\mathrm{SEP}}
is separable with respect to the bipartition
S_{1}\cdots S_{j}:S_{j+1}\cdots S_{R}
, the claim (
370
) follows.
∎

Theorem
8.2
now follows by applying the reduction from a general protocol
\pi
to an entanglement-based protocol
\pi^{\prime}
.

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Suppose
\pi
is a depth-
\Delta
-circuit which
generates distance-
R
-entanglement between two qubits using
m
qubits per site. Without loss of generality (see the remark above), assume that all two-qubit gates between sites are
\mathsf{CNOT}
gates. Let
t
be the maximal number of nearest-neighbor
\mathsf{CNOT}
gates used between two neighboring sites. Then
t\leq\Delta m
.

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be the entanglement-based protocol which uses the resource state
\Phi^{\otimes t}
.
Then, according to Lemma
8.3
, there is a local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p^{2})
acting on each half of the Bell states in the initial state (
364
) such that
\pi^{\prime}
fails with probability

\displaystyle p_{\textrm{fail}}\geq 1-e^{-R(4p^{2}/3)^{t}}\geq 1-e^{-R(4p^{2}/3)^{\Delta m}}\ .

(377)

By property (
iii
) of the entanglement-based protocol
\pi^{\prime}
, the local stochastic error
E\sim\mathcal{N}(p^{2})
on the resource state of
\pi^{\prime}

is equivalent to (a certain) local stochastic noise of strength
\mathcal{N}(p)
in the execution of
\pi
. The claim follows from this.
∎

8.2
A converse bound on cluster-state based schemes

Corollary
8.1
shows that
any fault-tolerant low-latency scheme for entanglement generation requires

\displaystyle m\geq\Omega(\log R)

(378)

qubits per repeater. For our cluster-state based scheme, the number
m
of qubits per repeater is given by
m=\Theta(d^{2})
, and our achievability result requires
d=\Omega(\log R)
. Thus our scheme works with

\displaystyle m=\Theta(\log^{2}R)

(379)

qubits per site.

Comparing (
378
) with (
379
), we observe a gap between our general no-go result and the achievability statement of Corollary
8.1
. In particular, (
378
) leaves room for an improved achievability result, either by a better analysis of our scheme or by considering different schemes.

In this section, we show that, in fact, the
scaling (
379
) resulting for the cluster-state based scheme is optimal, i.e., our analysis is tight. We also find that
a better scaling cannot be obtained by modifying the classical decoding functions only: Any scheme which
uses the cluster state
W\ket{0^{\cC}}
with the lattice
\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]
, applies the same measurement pattern and relies on the same syndrome information (associated with observed boundaries on the decoding graph)
requires at least
m=\Omega(\log^{2}R)
qubits per repeater (i.e., within any plane with
u_{3}
fixed).

Theorem 8.4

(Converse bound for cluster-state based schemes)
.

Let
0<p\leq\frac{1}{4}
. Consider an (infinite) family of pairs
(d,R)
such that
d
is even and
R
satisfies

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{p}}\right)^{d}\ .

(380)

Suppose we run Algorithm
2
on the input state
EW\ket{0^{\cC}}
in the cluster lattice
\mathcal{C}[d\times d\times R]
which is corrupted by i.i.d. single-qubit bit-flip noise with parameter
p
, i.e.,
E=\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}}X_{c}^{y_{c}}
where
\{y_{c}\}_{c\in\mathcal{C}}
are independent and identically distributed Bernoulli-
p
random variables, i.e.,
\Pr[y_{c}=1]=p
. Then the probability
\nu_{(0,0)}
of successful entanglement generation satisfies

\displaystyle\nu_{(0,0)}\leq 3/4\qquad\textrm{ for all sufficiently large }d\ .

(381)

The same statement applies to any scheme obtained from Algorithm on
2
by replacing the function
\mathsf{MinMatch}
by an (arbitrary) binary function.

Proof.

We first argue that the conclusions of this Theorem hold for our protocol. Recall the definition (
269
) of the operator
E_{\mathrm{gl}}
for any Pauli operator
E
on
\mathcal{C}
.
For the bit-flip noise model we consider here, there are no Pauli-
Z
errors (i.e.,
E^{Z}=0
), and thus

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{gl}}

\displaystyle=E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}}=\prod_{c\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}}X^{y_{c}}_{c}\ .

(382)

It follows that the two random variables
A_{Z}
and
B_{Z}
defined in (
341
) are given by

\displaystyle A_{Z}

\displaystyle=0

(383)

\displaystyle B_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\ .

(384)

Eq. (
342
)
implies that

\displaystyle 1-\nu_{(0,0)}

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[(A_{Z},B_{Z})=(0,1)\right]

(385)

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]\ ,

(386)

where we used (
383
), i.e., the fact that
A_{Z}=0
with probability
1
. Our claim thus follows if we establish the lower bound

\displaystyle\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]\geq 1/4\ .

(387)

Because of (
384
) and the definition of our noise model, we may express
the probability of interest as

\displaystyle\Pr\left[B_{Z}=1\right]=\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\right]

(388)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(b)}:=\left\{y\subseteq\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\ |\ \left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{(y\cap\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(y\cap\mathcal{A}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=b\right\}\qquad\textrm{ for }b=0,1\ .

(389)

(We will need
\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
below.)
Here
Y=\{Y_{e}\}_{e\in\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with parameter
p
. This is because
we have
Y=\mathrm{supp}(E^{X}|_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}\cap\mathcal{A}})
by definition of the noise model.

We will prove (
387
) by
showing that there is a subset
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}
that has probability at least

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1/4

(390)

under this distribution.

To construct
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
let us call a subset
y\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}

appropriate if it contains exactly
d/2
edges of any length-
d
, i.e., shortest-length path connecting the left and the right faces.
We denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
the set of appropriate subsets of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\subset\mathcal{E}^{(1)}

(391)

as the set of all
y\in\mathcal{E}
that are appropriate. To state this formally, let
e_{1}
be the first canonical basis vector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For any
0\leq j\leq d-1
and
1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}
, consider the two
external vertices
u_{j}^{k}
and
v_{j}^{k}
of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
defined in (
314
) and (
316
). We then consider the path
P_{j,k}\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
obtained by traversing sequence of vertices

\displaystyle u_{j}^{k},u_{j}^{k}+2e_{1},u_{j}^{k}+4e_{1},\ldots,u_{j}^{k}+2(d-1)e_{1},u_{j}^{k}+2de_{1}=v_{j}^{k}\ .

(392)

The path
P_{j,k}
is the shortest path (of length
d
) connecting two endpoints
u_{j}^{k}
and
v_{j}^{k}
.
A subset
y\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
is then called
appropriate (i.e.,
y\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
) if there are
(j,k)
with
0\leq j\leq d-1
and
1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}
such that

\displaystyle|y\cap P_{j,k}|=d/2\ .

(393)

It remains to find a lower bound on

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]\ .

(394)

In order to do so, we use the following observation.

Lemma 8.5
.

Consider the sets
\mathcal{E}^{(0)},\mathcal{E}^{(1)}
(cf. (
389
)) and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
. Let
b\in\{0,1\}
be arbitrary.
Then the following holds.

(i)

For every
y\in\mathcal{E}^{(b)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
, there is an element
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{E}^{(b\oplus 1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
such that

|y|=|\widehat{y}|
, i.e.,
y
and
\widehat{y}
have the same Hamming weight.

(ii)

The map
y\mapsto\widehat{y}
is injective.

Proof.

We give the proof for
b=0
(the case
b=1
is analogous). That is, consider
y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
. Because
y
is appropriate, there
is a – not necessarily unique – path
P_{j,k}

such that (
393
) holds.
In the following, we impose uniqueness by
lexicographically ordering the set of pairs
(j,k)
with

0\leq j\leq d-1
and
1\leq k\leq\frac{R-1}{2}
, and using the first (lowest) such pair
(j,k)
with property (
393
). We then define

\displaystyle\widehat{y}:=y\oplus P_{j,k}

(395)

as the symmetric difference of
y
and
P_{j,k}
. It is easy to check that the map
y\mapsto\widehat{y}
is well-defined.

Because
|P_{j,k}|=d
, Eq. (
393
) and the definition of
\widehat{y}
imply that
|\widehat{y}|=d/2
. In particular, we have
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
.
With this property and the fact that the paths
\{P_{j,k}\}_{j,k}
are pairwise disjoint, it is easy to see that the map
y\mapsto\widehat{y}
has a left-inverse (obtained by applying the same construction again). Hence it is injective.

It remains to show that
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}
for
y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
.
To this end, observe first that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\widehat{y}\cap\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(y\cap\mathcal{A})\ .

(396)

Eq. (
396
) follows
from the linearity of the boundary operator and the fact that the boundary of path is given by its endpoints. In our case, the endpoints of
P_{j,k}
are the external vertices
\{u_{j}^{k},v_{j}^{k}\}
of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, but these do not contribute to the boundary
\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}P_{j,k}
by definition of
\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}
.
This establishes (
396
).

We also use the following property of the path
P_{j,k}
: We have

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{P_{j,k}\cap\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle=1\

(397)

since the subset
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}
lies in a plane perpendicular to
P_{j,k}
with a single intersecting edge. Combining (
396
) and (
397
), and using linearity, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{(\widehat{y}\cap\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\widehat{y}\cap\mathcal{A}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hskip-3.01385pt\left\langle{(y\cap\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathsf{MinMatch}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(\partial_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(y\cap\mathcal{A}))},{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}}\right\rangle\hskip-3.01385pt\right\rangle\oplus 1

(398)

\displaystyle=0\oplus 1

(399)

\displaystyle=1

(400)

where we used the assumption that
y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}
in the penultimate step. This shows that
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}
as claimed.
∎

Returning to the problem of bounding (
394
), we observe that

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}}P_{Y}(y)

(401)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}}P_{Y}(\widehat{y})

(402)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}}P_{Y}(y)

(403)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]\ ,

(404)

where we used that
P_{Y}(y)
only depends on the Hamming weight
|y|
of
y
, and
|\widehat{y}|=|y|
. We also used that
\widehat{y}\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
and the fact that the map
y\mapsto\widehat{y}
is injective to obtain the inequality. By symmetry, i.e., interchanging
0
and
1
, we also have the converse inequality and thus the identity

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]\ .

(405)

(In fact, it is possible to construct a Hamming-weight-preserving bijection between the two sets
\mathcal{E}^{(b)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}
,
b=0,1
, but we do not need this here.)

Because the sets
\mathcal{E}^{(0)},\mathcal{E}^{(1)}
are disjoint, and the union is the set of all subsets of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]+\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]\right)

(406)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]\ .

(407)

We have

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

\displaystyle=1-\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\not\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

(408)

\displaystyle=1-\Pr_{Y}\left[|Y\cap P_{j,k}|\neq\frac{d}{2}\textrm{ for all }(j,k)\right]

(409)

\displaystyle=1-\left(1-\binom{d}{d/2}(2p)^{d/2}(1-2p)^{d/2}\right)^{d\cdot\frac{R-1}{2}}\ .

(410)

In the last step, we used the fact that
Y
consists of independent random Bernoulli variables, and the fact that the paths
\{P_{j,k}\}_{j,k}
are pairwise disjoint.

By Stirling’s formula

\binom{2k}{k}=\left(1+o(1)\right)\frac{2^{2k}}{\sqrt{\pi k}}
for
k\rightarrow\infty
and the inequality
2p(1-2p)\geq p
for
p\leq\frac{1}{4}
we obtain

\displaystyle\binom{d}{d/2}(2p)^{d/2}(1-2p)^{d/2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1+o(1)\right)\frac{2^{d}}{\sqrt{\pi d/2}}p^{d/2}

(411)

\displaystyle\geq 0.3\frac{(2\sqrt{p})^{d}}{\sqrt{d}}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad d\textrm{ sufficiently large}\ .

(412)

We have

\displaystyle(1-x)^{r}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-rx}\qquad\textrm{ for all }\qquad x\in(0,1)\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad r\in\mathbb{N}\ ,

(413)

hence

\displaystyle\left(1-\binom{d}{d/2}(2p)^{d/2}(1-2p)^{d/2}\right)^{d\cdot\frac{R-1}{2}}\leq\exp\left(-0.15\sqrt{d}\cdot(R-1)\cdot(2\sqrt{p})^{d}\right)\ .

(414)

We conclude with (
410
) and (
407
) that

\displaystyle\Pr_{Y}\left[Y\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{appr}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\exp\left(-0.15\sqrt{d}\cdot(R-1)\cdot(2\sqrt{p})^{d}\right)\right)

(415)

\displaystyle\geq 1/4

(416)

for sufficiently large
d
if

\displaystyle\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{d}\cdot(R-1)\cdot(2\sqrt{p})^{d}=\infty\ .

(417)

Condition (
417
) is satisfied when (
380
) holds.
In particular, we obtain the claim (
390
) for sufficiently large
d
.

It remains to show that this argument also applies to protocols where the matching function
\mathsf{MinMatch}
is replaced by a different function in Algorithm
2
. This is because – as discussed at the end of Section
6
– Theorem
6.6
does not require
\mathsf{MinMatch}
to return a matching of a given input. Neither does (
342
), which is obtained from Theorem
6.6
.

∎

Appendix A
Products of stabilizer generators of the graph state

In this appendix, we give proofs of
Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
concerning products of stabilizer generators of the cluster state.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Let
\left(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\right)
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

(i)

Consider an element
u=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with
u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}
, i.e.,
u\in\mathsf{V}_{\mathrm{even}}
with
\mathrm{neigh}(u)\subset\mathsf{E}_{\mathrm{even}}
.

By definition, any
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
has the form

\displaystyle v=u+s_{j}e_{j}\qquad\textrm{ with }\qquad j\in\{1,2,3\}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad s_{j}\in\{-1,1\}\ .

(418)

Moreover, for any such
v=u+s_{j}e_{j}\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
, any
w\in\mathrm{neigh}(v)
takes the form

\displaystyle w=u+s_{j}e_{j}+s_{k}e_{k}\qquad\textrm{ with }\qquad k\in\{1,2,3\}\ ,k\neq j\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad s_{k}\in\{-1,1\}\ .

(419)

This is because
\mathrm{neigh}(v)\subset\mathcal{C}
does not contain elements of the form
(e,e,e)
, and thus
u\not\in\mathrm{neigh}(v)
.

It follows from the definition (
242
) of
S^{u}
for

u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with
u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}
that

\displaystyle S^{u}

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}\left(Z_{v}\prod_{w\in\mathrm{neigh}(v)}X_{w}\right)

(420)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}Z_{v}\right)\Bigl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w):\\
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)\\
w\in\mathrm{neigh}(v)\end{subarray}}X_{w}\Bigr)\ .

(421)

Here we used that for any two distinct
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
, we have
v\not\in\mathrm{neigh}(v^{\prime})
(as follows immediately from (
418
)), implying that the product (
420
) does not contain a factor
X_{v}
.

Now suppose that
v,w
are such that
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
and
w\in\mathrm{neigh}(v)
. Assume that
v
and
w
are of the form (
418
) and (
419
), respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=u+s_{j}e_{j}

(422)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=u+s_{j}+s_{k}e_{k}\ .

(423)

Consider

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=u+s_{k}e_{k}\ .

(424)

Then it follows from
u\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
and
v,w\in\mathcal{C}
that
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)

(425)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{neigh}(v^{\prime})\ .

(426)

This shows that associated with every pair
(v,w)
giving rise to a factor
X_{w}
in the product (
421
), there is a pair
(v^{\prime},w)\neq(v,w)
also contributing a factor
X_{w}
. Thus all such factors cancel and we are left with

\displaystyle S^{u}

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)}Z_{v}=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)}Z_{v}\

(427)

because
\mathrm{neigh}(u)=\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)
by definition of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
. In particular, we have
\mathrm{supp}(S^{u})=\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)
as claimed.
Let us argue that for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with
u_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{neigh}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\subset\mathcal{A}

(428)

(and thus in particular
\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
). Observe that
combining (
427
) with (
428
) implies the claim (
243
) for
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with
u_{3}\notin\{1,R\}
.
To show (
428
), consider
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
of the form
v=u+s_{j}e_{j}
.
Suppose that
v_{3}\in\{1,R\}
. Then this implies that either

\displaystyle u_{3}=2,j=3,s_{j}=-1\qquad\textrm{ or }\qquad u_{3}=R-1,j=3,s_{j}=1\ .

(429)

In both cases, it follows that
v\in\{(e,e,o)\}
(since
u\in\{(e,e,e)\}
) and thus
v\in\mathcal{A}
.
This implies (
428
) since any
v\in\mathrm{neigh}(u)
with
v_{3}\not\in\{1,R\}
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

Consider an element
u\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
with
u_{3}\in\{1,R\}
.
We claim that

\displaystyle\{u\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{A}

(430)

\displaystyle\mathrm{neigh}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)\cap\mathcal{B}\ .

Note that the claim (
243
) follows from (
430
) combined with the definition of
G_{u}
.
Observe that
u
is incident to an edge

\displaystyle\{u,v\}\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad v=\begin{cases}(u_{1},u_{2},2)&{ if $u_{3}=1$}\\
(u_{1},u_{2},R-1)&{ if $u_{3}=R$} \ .\end{cases}

(431)

To describe the edges in the subgraph
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{u_{3}\}
, let us define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\mid w_{3}=u_{3}\}

(432)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{u_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathcal{C}\mid w_{3}=u_{3}\}\ .

(433)

Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{3}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{u_{3}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the set of two-dimensional sites and the set of locations of qubits introduced in Section
3.1
, respectively.
For a site
w=(w_{1},w_{2},u_{3})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{3}}
, we denote by
\mathrm{neigh}_{u_{3}}(w)
the set of nearest neighbors of
w
in
\mathcal{C}_{u_{3}}
. More explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{neigh}_{u_{3}}(w)=\left\{z\in\mathcal{C}_{u_{3}}\mid\sum_{j=1}^{2}\absolutevalue{w_j - z_j}=1\right\}\ .

(434)

From the definition (
239
) of
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, it follows that
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathrm{dec}}
.
Then it follows from the definition (
54
) that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{u_{3}\}}(u)=\mathrm{neigh}_{u_{3}}(u)\ ,

(435)

where each sites in
\mathrm{neigh}_{u_{3}}(u)
is the midpoint of an edge of the lattice
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{u_{3}\}
.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{neigh}_{u_{3}}(u)=\mathrm{neigh}(u)\ .

(436)

By construction of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
, the edge
\{u,v\}
from (
431
) and the edges in
\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\times\{u_{3}\}}
form the complete set of the edges where
u
is incident in
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inci}_{\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{dec}}}(u)=\mathrm{neigh}(u)\cup\{u\}.

(437)

The claim (
430
) follows from (
437
) and the fact that
u\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathrm{neigh}(u)\subset\mathcal{B}
.

The cases for the dual decoding graph can be checked in analogous manner.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We show the claim (
247
).
Let
u=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B}
. Then
u_{1}=1
, and
u_{2}
,
u_{3}
are even.
The stabilizer generator
G_{u}
is

\displaystyle G_{u}=\begin{cases}X_{(1,u_{2},u_{3}-1)}Z_{(1,u_{2},u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2}+1,u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2},u_{3}+1)}&{ if $u_{2}=0$}\\
X_{(1,u_{2},u_{3}-1)}Z_{(1,u_{2},u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2}+1,u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2}-1,u_{3})}X_{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}+1)}&{ if $2\leq u_{2}\leq 2d-4$}\\
X_{(1,u_{2},u_{3}-1)}Z_{(1,u_{2},u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2}-1,u_{3})}X_{(1,u_{2},u_{3}+1)}&{ if $u_{2}=2d-2$} \ .\end{cases}

(438)

Define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\prod_{k=2,4,\dots,R-1}Z_{(1,0,k)}X_{(1,1,k)}&{ if $j=0$}\\
\prod_{k=2,4,\dots,R-1}Z_{(1,j,k)}X_{(1,j-1,k)}X_{(1,j+1,k)}&{ if $j=2,4,\dots,2d-4$}\\
\prod_{k=2,4,\dots,R-1}Z_{(1,r-1,k)}X_{(1,r-2,k)}&{ if $j=2d-2$} \ .\end{cases}

(439)

Then it is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\prod_{k=2,4,\dots,R-1}G_{(1,j,k)}=X_{(1,j,1)}\mathcal{G}_{j}X_{(1,j,R)}\qquad\textrm{ for }\qquad j=0,2,\dots,2d-2\ .

(440)

We observe that for all even
j,j^{\prime}
and
u_{3}\in\{1,R\}
, the site
(1,j,u_{3})
does not appear in any of the factors in the product
\mathcal{G}_{j^{\prime}}
in (
439
).
This implies that
X_{(1,j,u_{3})}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j^{\prime}}
commute, and thus

\displaystyle S^{X}

\displaystyle=\prod_{u\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{X}}G_{u}

(441)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}\left(\prod_{k=2,4,\dots,R-1}G_{(1,j,k)}\right)

(442)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}X_{(1,j,1)}\mathcal{G}_{j}X_{(1,j,R)}

(443)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}X_{(1,j,1)}X_{(1,j,R)}\right)\left(\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}\mathcal{G}_{j}\right)

(444)

\displaystyle=X_{q_{1}}X_{q_{2}}X(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{B})\left(\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}\mathcal{G}_{j}\right)\ .

(445)

It is easy to check that in
\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}\mathcal{G}_{j}
all single-qubit Pauli operators commute and that all Pauli-
X
are cancelled.
As a result, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0,2,\dots,2d-2}\mathcal{G}_{j}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0,2,\dots,2d-2\\
k=2,4,\dots,R-1\end{subarray}}Z_{(1,j,k)}=Z(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cl},X}\cap\mathcal{A})\ .

(446)

The claim (
247
) follows from (
445
) and (
446
).
The claim (
248
) can be checked in analogous manner.
∎

Appendix B
An upper bound on the resilience function

\mathsf{res}_{\mathcal{L}_{Z}}(p)

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

We label the external vertices of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}}=\{u_{1},\dots,u_{d},v_{1},\dots,v_{d-1}\}\qquad\textrm{with}\qquad u_{j}=(2j-1,2j-1)\textrm{ and }v_{k}=(2k+1,-1)\ ,

(447)

see Fig.
7b

Clearly, a simple path
P\in Z_{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathrm{dec}^{*}})
with external endpoints through internal vertices satisfies
